Cosmological Collider Signatures from Right-Handed Neutrino Loop
Jingtao You, Linghao Song, Chengcheng Han, Hong-Jian He, Xingang Chen, Zhong-Zhi Xianyu

Abstract
We study cosmological collider (CC) signatures generated by right-handed neutrino loops in the setup of inflation combined with neutrino seesaw mechanism. We formulate the inflaton interaction with the right-handed neutrino through a unique dimension-5 operator respecting shift symmetry, which induces an effective chemical potential in the slow-roll background, leading to helicity-dependent right-handed-neutrino production and enhancing the CC signatures. The right-handed neutrino is described by two-component Weyl spinor with Majorana mass term. Using the Schwinger-Keldysh (SK) formalism, we derive a set of seed integrals for fermion propagators of the right-handed Majorana neutrino. With these we compute the factorized nonlocal contributions to the three-point inflaton correlator generated by the right-handed-neutrino triangle loop. We show that the chemical potential can substantially soften the heavy-mass Boltzmann suppression and amplify the oscillatory non-Gaussianity signatures associated with the dominant helicity mode. These provide a systematic framework for analyzing the fermion loop signatures in the cosmological collider physics and demonstrate that the heavy right-handed neutrinos associated with seesaw mechanism may leave observable imprints in the primordial non-Gaussianities.



1
Introduction

Recent studies have highlighted the inflationary universe and its quantum fluctuations
62
;
34
;
41
;
42
;
49
as an important arena for probing high-energy particle interactions.
Unlike terrestrial experiments that are limited by achievable center-of-mass energies, inflation is sensitive to particles with masses comparable to the Hubble scale
H
. If such heavy states are present and interact with the inflaton, they can leave characteristic imprints on primordial curvature perturbations and the corresponding correlation functions. In particular, higher-point correlators of primordial fluctuations may exhibit non-analytic momentum-dependence in special kinematic limits, thereby encoding direct information about the masses, spins, and couplings of the particles present during inflation. This observation underlies the program of
cosmological collider physics

24
;
25
;
14
;
51
;
10
;
26
;
53
;
33
;
19
;
40
;
22
;
38
;
39
, in which the primordial non-Gaussianities
27
;
68
;
47
;
1
are used as a spectroscopic probe of ultraviolet physics in the early universe. Current CMB measurements have already placed constraints on the non-Gaussianity parameter at the level of

f_{\mathrm{NL}}\!\lesssim\!{O}(10)
, depending on the shape of the non-Gaussian signal. Future observations of large-scale structure may push this sensitivity down to

{O}(1)\!-\!{O}(0.1)
, and potentially to
{O}(0.01)
with 21cm tomography
50
;
46
;
47
;
1
.

A central prediction of cosmological collider physics is that massive fields can generate oscillatory or non-analytic power-law signals in momentum-space correlators of primordial fluctuations.
Schematically, in the squeezed limit
k_{L}\!\ll\!k_{S}\hskip 0.85358pt
, one expects the 3pt correlator of the primordial curvature perturbation
\zeta
to take the form
25
;
51
;
10
,

\langle\zeta^{3}\rangle\simeq B_{\zeta}(k_{L},k_{S})\sim\mathcal{A}(k_{L},k_{S})\!\hskip-0.85358pt\left(\!\!\frac{~k_{L}\,}{\,k_{S}\,}\hskip-0.85358pt\!\right)^{\!\!\alpha+\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\omega}\!+\mathrm{c.c.}\,,

(1)

where the oscillation frequency or power-law exponent set by
\omega
is determined by the heavy-particle spectrum. Such non-analytic dependence on the momentum ratio
k_{L}/k_{S}
cannot be generated
by local effective interactions of light fields alone, and thus constitutes a distinctive signature
of particle production and propagation during the inflation. For this reason, these features are often referred to as the
cosmological collider signals
or

clock signals
. Hence, the detection of such cosmological collider signals would open up a new window to probing physics
at energy scales far beyond those accessible in the laboratory experiments.

Realizing the goal of quantitatively probing physics at the inflation scale requires theoretical control over
the relevant inflationary correlators. Similar to flat-space scattering amplitudes, inflationary correlators can be computed perturbatively with a Feynman-diagram expansion. In recent years, much progress has been made in analytic treatments of scalar-mediated cosmological-collider signals. These developments mainly rely on a detailed understanding of scalar integrals associated with bulk scalar propagators. The tree-level processes were first tackled with a differential-equation-based
approach
9
;
11
;
12
;
13
,
and the full tree-level solutions have been found
later
72
;
30
;
44
;
73
. At the loop level, some new techniques have been developed for analytic integration,
with a focus on the oscillatory signals, including partial or complete Mellin-Barnes representations
60
;
54
;
56
;
59
,
spectral decomposition
74
;
77
, dispersion relations
43
;
71
,
and the factorization theorems and cutting rules for nonlocal signals
63
;
57
;
58
;
28
. These developments have improved both the conceptual understanding and the computational method
of scalar cosmological collider observables.

In contrast, the studies on computing fermion-loop contributions remain very limited. This is because fermion loops involve nontrivial spinor structures, helicity dependence, and a more intricate analytic structure
than scalar loops. These make the quantitative treatment of fermion loops highly challenging. Some previous studies investigated heavy-fermion loop contributions to the primordial
non-Gaussianity
23
;
37
;
36
;
45
;
8
, but these
calculations often rely on strong assumptions that oversimplify the complexity of fermion-loop integrals. In particular, existing estimates often adopt a saddle-point approximation
for the hard internal propagator and infer the loop contribution
from its characteristic nonanalytic soft-momentum scaling,
rather than evaluating the full loop integral explicitly. While such approximations might capture the exponential parametric dependence,
the associated power-law behaviors are still poorly understood. Hence, a more precise treatment of the fermion-loop integrals is necessary
for reliable signal predictions. In this work, we perform a computation of the cosmological collider signal from the three-point inflationary correlator
induced by fermion loops. With a systematic treatment of the spinor and helicity structures and combining the recent
techniques of scalar-loop integrals, we derive a controlled formula including both the exponential dependence
and associated power-law dependence on the mass and chemical potential.

In addition to the technical difficulty, there is also an important question regarding the observability. For a heavy state with mass
m\!\gtrsim\!H
, the particle production
in the de Sitter space is typically suppressed exponentially, and this suppression
is inherited by the nonlocal signal
through an approximate Boltzmann factor,

\exp(-\pi m/H)\hskip 0.85358pt.

(2)

As a result, even when heavy particles generate distinctive oscillatory signatures, the overall amplitude
could be too small to be observable if their production rate is strongly suppressed. A particularly interesting possibility for fermions is that this suppression can be substantially reduced
in the presence of a
chemical potential

23
;
37
;
36
;
66
;
65
;
67
;
18
;
61
;
64
;
56
;
17
;
16
;
8
. In an inflationary background, derivative couplings between the inflaton and heavy fields can induce
an effective chemical potential through the time-dependent inflaton background. For fermions, such a
coupling may schematically take the following form,

\frac{1}{\,\Lambda\,}\,\partial_{\mu}\phi\,J^{\mu},

(3)

where
J^{\mu}
is a chiral fermionic current and

\Lambda
denotes the cutoff scale of the effective inflaton-fermion interaction. Once the inflaton develops a homogeneous slow-roll background,
\dot{\phi}_{0}\!\neq\!0\hskip 0.85358pt
,
this interaction generates an effective chemical potential,

\lambda\sim\frac{\,\dot{\phi}_{0}\,}{\,\Lambda\,}.

(4)

Physically speaking, the chemical potential induces helicity-dependent fermion production: one helicity state
can be exponentially enhanced, while the other is suppressed. For sufficiently large
\lambda
, production of the dominant helicity mode can be significantly amplified, weakening the usual Boltzmann suppression and thereby enhancing the observational prospects for fermionic cosmological collider signals.

To demonstrate how this possibility is explicitly realized,
it is important to identify a well-motivated new physics candidate of heavy fermions from particle physics. In this work, we take the heavy right-handed neutrinos with the canonical seesaw
mechanism
48
;
31
;
75
;
32

as a compelling new physics benchmark for this approach, where the seesaw mechanism naturally explains
the tiny masses of the light neutrinos as observed by the neutrino oscillation experiments and the right-handed neutrinos
provide the last missing piece in the structure of the Standard Model (SM) of particle physics. We note
76
;
35
that the scale of the canonical neutrino seesaw
is naturally of
O(10^{13-14})
GeV, lying around the upper range of the inflation energy scale. When the heavy right-handed neutrinos couple to the inflaton through a unique derivative operator of
dimension-5
23
;
66
;
76
;
35
,
the rolling inflaton background can induce a chemical potential
for the right-handed neutrinos and enhance their production during inflation. In this way, the right-handed neutrinos (with the canonical seesaw mechanism) serve as
a unique heavy fermion candidate and can be tested by studying the cosmological collider physics.

Figure 1
:
Illustration of the primordial non-Gaussianity observable from the three-point inflaton correlator
as generated by the heavy-neutrino triangle loop during the inflation epoch and as (to be) measured
in the present universe.

This setup also provides an important complement to the commonly studied bosonic cosmological collider signals. The heavy fermion signals originate from the propagation of heavy fermions in the loop and manifest themselves through non-analytic structures in appropriate kinematic limits of the cosmological correlator. The oscillation frequency probes the heavy-fermion mass spectrum, whereas the overall oscillation amplitude depends on the strength of the inflaton coupling and the size of the induced chemical potential. Hence, the loop-induced fermionic cosmological collider signals can encode the information about both the heavy fermion mass-spectrum and their underlying interaction dynamics.

With these motivations, in this work we study the cosmological collider signatures generated
by heavy right-handed neutrino loops through the inflaton-right-handed-neutrino interaction,
where the right-handed neutrinos join to realize the canonical neutrino seesaw mechanism. We will analyze how the right-handed neutrino loops modify the primordial correlation functions through their interaction with the inflaton. A key ingredient of our analysis is the chemical potential induced by the slow-roll inflaton background. We demonstrate that this chemical potential can exponentially enhance the production of one of the fermion helicity states and significantly amplify the resultant oscillatory signals in the primordial non-Gaussianity. Our analysis uses the two-component Weyl spinors and the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism, together with a factorized treatment of the loop diagram and a set of seed integrals for the fermion propagators. This provides a general framework for heavy fermion loop signals in cosmological collider physics and demonstrates that the heavy right-handed neutrinos
(whose masses set the seesaw scale) may leave observable imprints in the primordial non-Gaussianity, as illustrated in Fig.
1
.

In general, the full clock signal can receive both nonlocal and local contributions. The nonlocal contribution originates from the long-distance propagation of gravitationally
produced massive particles between separated bulk vertices and gives a characteristic
nonanalytic dependence on the external momenta in the soft limit. The local contribution
arises from the short-distance part of the bulk dynamics. Although it may also contain
oscillatory clock dependence, its coefficient and phase are controlled
by short-distance dynamics and local interactions.
In the three-point squeezed limit, these two contributions can provide the same oscillatory
momentum dependence and thus may interfere at the level of the total amplitude. For the computational part of this work, we perform an improved computation of
the nonlocal contributions from the fermion loops,
including computation of each factorized hard subdiagram and bubble-loop subdiagram. These improvements show the importance of reliable control over
the power-law parametric dependence in the final results,
which can lead to order-of-magnitude changes from the previous estimates for signals
with very large oscillation frequencies. On the other hand, the local contribution is technically difficult to compute. This is because the local contribution is controlled by short-distance regions
of the bulk time integrals, rather than by the long-distance propagation of
massive particles alone. A reliable calculation requires the full unfactorized time integrals
and a careful treatment of the associated local terms. Since there is no known reason indicating that including the local contribution would
significantly reduce the total signal through large cancellations with nonlocal component,
our computed nonlocal contribution could be viewed as a conservative estimate for
the observable clock amplitude. A quantitative treatment of the local contribution
is technically more challenging and is worth to be tackled for the future work.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce the inflationary setup with right-handed neutrinos, derive the effective chemical potential induced by the derivative coupling of inflaton and righ-handed neutrinos, and present the fermion propagators and the associated seed integrals in the inflationary background. In Section
3
, we compute the three-point inflaton correlator generated by the right-handed-neutrino loops and extract the non-analytic cosmological collider signals. In Section
4
, we analyze the dependence of the cosmological collider signals on the heavy fermion mass, chemical potential, and coupling parameters. With these, we present estimates for the
three-point non-Gaussian amplitude
f_{\mathrm{NL}}
. Finally, we summarize our conclusions in Section
5
. In Appendices
A
-
C
, we provide the technical derivations as needed for the analyses in the main text. In Appendix
D
, we give a brief review to describe the main concepts and ingredients of the cosmological collider physics
which allows the current presentation accessible to a wider readership.

Notation and Conventions:

We consider that the background spacetime is the inflationary patch of de Sitter spacetime, which provides the
standard approximation to the slow-roll inflation. We use the coordinates
(\tau,\mathbf{x})
, where

\tau\!\in\!(-\infty,0)
is the conformal time and
\mathbf{x}
denotes the comoving spatial coordinates. The metric tensor is given by the distance-square,

\text{d}s^{2}=a^{2}(\tau)(\hskip-0.85358pt-\text{d}\tau^{2}\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\text{d}\mathbf{x}^{2})=\frac{\,-\text{d}\tau^{2}\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\text{d}\mathbf{x}^{2}\,}{(H\tau)^{2}}\,,

(5)

where
a(\tau)\!=\!-1/(H\tau)
is the scale factor in de Sitter spacetime, and the Hubble parameter
H
(also denoted as
H_{\mathrm{inf}}
) during inflation is taken to be constant. For any functions
f(\tau)
and
\phi(t)
, we denote the time-derivatives

f^{\prime}(\tau)\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\text{d}f(\tau)/\text{d}\tau\hskip 0.85358pt

and
\dot{\phi}(t)\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\text{d}\phi(t)/\text{d}t\hskip 0.85358pt
.

Three-dimensional vectors will be denoted in boldface, for example, a spatial momentum is written as
\mathbf{k}

and its magnitude is defined as
k\!=\!|\mathbf{k}|
. For clarity, some definitions of the momentum variables and momentum ratios
(used in the following presentation) are given as follows:

\displaystyle k_{s}\hskip-0.85358pt\equiv\hskip-0.85358pt\lvert\mathbf{k}_{1}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{2}\rvert\hskip 0.85358pt,~~~k_{t}\hskip-0.85358pt\equiv\hskip-0.85358pt\lvert\mathbf{k}_{1}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{4}\rvert\hskip 0.85358pt,~~~k_{ij}\hskip-0.85358pt\equiv\hskip-0.85358ptk_{i}\!+\!k_{j}\hskip 0.85358pt,~~~r_{1}\hskip-0.85358pt\equiv\hskip-0.85358ptk_{s}/k_{12}\hskip 0.85358pt,~~~r_{2}\hskip-0.85358pt\equiv\hskip-0.85358ptk_{s}/k_{34}\hskip 0.85358pt,

(6)

where the subscripts
i,j=1,2,3,\cdots
.

The generalized hypergeometric function
{}_{p}F_{q}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{}_{p}F_{q}\left[\!\begin{array}[]{c}a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\ldots,b_{q}\end{array}\,\middle|\,z\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\!\frac{\,(a_{1})_{n}\cdots(a_{p})_{n}\,}{(b_{1})_{n}\cdots(b_{q})_{n}}\frac{\,z^{n}\,}{n!}\,,

where
(a)_{n}\!=\!\Gamma(a\!+\!n)/\Gamma(a)
is the Pochhammer symbol. The corresponding regularized hypergeometric function
{}_{p}\tilde{F}_{q}

is defined in the following formula:

\displaystyle{}_{p}\tilde{F}_{q}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\ldots,b_{q}\end{array}\,\middle|\,z\right]=\frac{1}{\,\Gamma[b_{1},\ldots,b_{q}]\,}\,{}_{p}F_{q}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\ldots,b_{q}\end{array}\,\middle|\,z\right]\!.

2
Setup of Inflation with Neutrino Seesaw

In the canonical neutrino seesaw mechanism, for a natural Yukawa coupling
y_{\nu}=O(1)
, the seesaw scale is typically around
10^{14}\,
GeV, which is a brand-new scale beyond the SM and can be naturally embedded
in the grand unification. Interestingly, this natural high-scale seesaw is also compatible with the upper range of the inflation scale
2
;
6
;
7
, characterized by the Hubble parameter during inflation,
H
. This observation motivates us to consider the appealing possibility that the inflaton field
\phi
couples directly to the right-handed neutrino
N
. Such a coupling could provide an intriguing opportunity to probe the natural seesaw mechanism through inflationary observables in the early universe.

In this section, we explore a natural interaction between the inflaton and right-handed neutrinos in a general slow-roll inflationary setup. We show that, in the presence of the inflaton background, this interaction alters the neutrino dynamics by generating an effective chemical potential. We also provide the fermion propagators in the Schwinger–Keldysh (SK) formalism, together with the associated seed integrals that will be employed in the computation of inflationary correlators in the following sections.

2.1
Inflaton Interaction with Right-Handed Neutrino

In many inflation models, the inflaton potential
V(\phi)
must be sufficiently flat to satisfy the slow-roll conditions
41
;
42
and agree with observations
2
;
6
;
7
;
3
. An approximate shift symmetry is often introduced to protect this flatness. As a result, the direct coupling between the inflaton and the right-handed neutrino,
y_{\phi}\phi(NN\!+\!\!\text{ H.c. }\!\!)
, is expected to be suppressed
1
1

1

The contraction of two Weyl spinors
\left(NN\right)
is defined by
\left(NN\equiv N^{\alpha}N_{\alpha}\right)
, in accordance with the convention adopted in Ref.
29
.
. If instead one considers a derivative coupling between the inflaton and the right-handed neutrino, the leading interaction naturally arises as a dimension-5 operator. The relevant Lagrangian in the Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker (FLRW) spacetime
(or the de Sitter spacetime)
2
2

2

We give a brief discussion about the Lagrangian of the Weyl spinors in curved spacetime
in the Appendix
A
.
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-g\,}\bigg[\hskip-0.85358pt\!-\!\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.85358pt\partial_{\mu}\phi\hskip 0.85358pt\partial^{\mu}\phi\hskip 0.85358pt-\!V(\phi)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{a(\tau)}N^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\text{D}_{\mu}N+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{a(\tau)}\nu_{\text{L}}^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\text{D}_{\mu}\nu_{\text{L}}

(13)

\displaystyle-\!\frac{1}{\,a(\tau)\Lambda\,}\partial_{\mu}\phi N^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}N+\!\left(\!\hskip-0.85358pt-\frac{1}{2}MN\!N\!-\frac{y_{\nu}h}{\sqrt{2}}\,\nu_{\text{L}}N+\!\!\text{ H.c. }\!\!\!\hskip-0.85358pt\right)\!\!\bigg]\hskip-0.85358pt.

In the above equation,
V(\phi)
denotes the inflaton potential, which is assumed to be sufficiently flat such that the inflaton can be treated as effectively massless during inflation. The fields
\nu_{\text{L}}
and
N
correspond to the left-handed and right-handed neutrinos, respectively, both of which are described using two-component Weyl spinors. The operator
\mathrm{D}_{\mu}
represents the covariant derivative acting on spinors in de Sitter spacetime. Finally,
h
denotes the remaining physical component of the Higgs field in the unitary gauge. The dimension-5 coupling can be expressed as follows:

\Delta\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{-g\,}\,\frac{-1}{a(\tau)\Lambda}\,\partial_{\mu}\phi\,N^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}N=a^{3}(\tau)\,\frac{-1}{\Lambda}\,\partial_{\mu}\phi\,N^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}N\,,

(14)

where we have used
\sqrt{-g\,}=a^{4}(\tau)
for de Sitter space in conformal coordinates. Here,
\partial_{\mu}\phi
denote derivatives with respect to conformal coordinates.

In the previous work
35
;
76
, we studied the prediction of local-type non-Gaussianity
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\text{local}}
generated through Higgs-modulated reheating in the neutrino seesaw framework. In the present work, we instead investigate the impact of the dimension-5 coupling on the quantum fluctuations of the inflaton. This effect is encoded in the cosmological collider signals induced by the derivative coupling between the inflaton and the right-handed neutrino during inflation. Before proceeding to the calculation, we first introduce the theoretical framework for fermions in an inflationary background.

2.2
Fermions in the Slow-Roll Inflationary Background

In Eq.(
13
), we introduce a minimal model of the canonical seesaw combined with inflaton. Within this scenario, the right-handed neutrino, with a Majorana mass term
M
, is coupled to the inflaton through a dimension-5 operator. In this section, we study the dynamics of the neutrino field during the inflation. We will illustrate that the right-handed neutrino would experience an effective chemical potential sourced from the inflaton background due to the dimension-5 coupling.

During inflation, the inflaton field
\phi(t,\mathbf{x})
is decomposed into a homogeneous background part
\phi_{0}(t)
and a quantum fluctuation
\delta\phi(t,\mathbf{x})
,

\phi(t,\mathbf{x})=\phi_{0}(t)+\delta\phi(t,\mathbf{x})\,.

(15)

Depending on the type of inflation models, the background inflaton field could be much larger or smaller than the Planck scale.
In a slow-roll background, the rolling rate of
\phi_{0}(t)
,
\dot{\phi}_{0}\equiv\partial_{t}{\phi}_{0}
with respect to the physical time
t
, satisfies the slow-roll condition and is also constrained by observations through the dimensionless power spectrum
P_{\zeta}

of the comoving curvature perturbation
\zeta
as follows:

P_{\zeta}=\frac{H^{4}}{\,4\pi^{2}\dot{\phi}_{0}^{2}\,}=\frac{H^{2}}{\,8\pi^{2}\epsilon M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}\,}\simeq 2\hskip-0.85358pt\times\!10^{-9}\,,

(16)

where the slow-roll parameter
\epsilon
is given by

\epsilon\equiv-\frac{\dot{H}}{\,H^{2}\,}=\frac{\dot{\phi}_{0}^{2}}{\,2H^{2}M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}\,}\,.

(17)

Using the observed amplitude of the scalar power spectrum, one finds that the rolling rate of the inflaton background is approximately given by

\dot{\phi}_{0}\simeq(60H)^{2}\,.

(18)

In this case, the dimension-5 coupling to the inflaton induces a nontrivial modification of the quadratic part of the neutrino Lagrangian. In particular,

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-g\,}\,\frac{-1}{\,a(\tau)\Lambda\,}\,\partial_{\mu}\phi\,N^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}N

\displaystyle\supset\sqrt{-g\,}\,\frac{-1}{\,a(\tau)\Lambda\,}\,\partial_{0}\phi_{0}\,N^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{0}N

\displaystyle=-a^{4}(\tau)\,\frac{\,\dot{\phi}_{0}\,}{\Lambda}\,N^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{0}N\,,

(19)

where
\partial_{0}\phi_{0}=\partial_{\tau}\phi_{0}(\tau)
denotes the derivative of the inflaton background
\phi_{0}(\tau)
with respect to conformal time
\tau
, while
\dot{\phi}_{0}
denotes the derivative with respect to physical time
t
(referred to as the rolling rate in the above discussion). These are related by
\partial_{0}\phi_{0}=a\,\dot{\phi}_{0}
. We may then write the quadratic terms for the right-handed neutrino in Eq.(
13
) as

\Delta\mathcal{L}=a^{3}N^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}D\!\!\!\!/\,N\!-\!a^{4}\lambda N^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{0}N\!-\!\frac{1}{2}a^{4}M_{\!R}(NN\!+\!\!\text{ H.c. }\!\!)\,,

(20)

where
M_{\!R}
is the heavy neutrino mass, and
\lambda\!\equiv\!\dot{\phi}_{0}/\Lambda
arises from the dimension-5 coupling between the inflaton and the right-handed neutrino and plays the role of an effective chemical potential for the right-handed neutrino. It is convenient to redefine the field as
N(x)\!=\!a^{-3/2}\tilde{N}(x)\hskip 0.85358pt
, so that the covariant derivative acting on
N
is reduced to an ordinary partial derivative acting on
\tilde{N}
. In terms of
\tilde{N}
, the above Lagrangian takes the following form:

\Delta\mathcal{L}=\tilde{N}^{\dagger}i\partial\!\!\!/\tilde{N}-a\lambda\tilde{N}^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{0}\tilde{N}-\frac{1}{2}aM_{\!R}(\tilde{N}\tilde{N}\!+\!\!\text{ H.c. }\!\!)\,.

(21)

The equation of motion is obtained by varying the Lagrangian with respect to
\tilde{N}^{\dagger}
, yielding

i\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{N}=a\lambda\bar{\sigma}^{0}\tilde{N}+aM_{\!R}\tilde{N}^{\dagger}\,.

(22)

Then we decompose the spinor
\tilde{N}
into eigenmodes of the three-momentum,

\displaystyle\tilde{N}_{\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\frac{\text{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{s=\pm}\!\left[\xi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})b_{s}(\mathbf{k})e^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\chi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})b_{s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})e^{-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\right]\!,

(23)

\displaystyle\tilde{N}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\frac{\text{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{s=\pm}\!\left[\xi^{\dagger}_{s,{\dot{\alpha}}}(\tau,\mathbf{k})b_{s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})e^{-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\chi^{\dagger}_{s,{\dot{\alpha}}}(\tau,\mathbf{k})b_{s}(\mathbf{k})e^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\right]\!,

where
s\!=\!\pm 1
denotes the sign of the spinor helicity, and
b_{s}
and
b_{s}^{\dagger}
are the annihilation and creation operators obeying the anticommutation relation,

\{b_{s}(\mathbf{k}),b_{s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k^{\prime}})\}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}\delta^{3}(\mathbf{k}\!-\!\mathbf{k^{\prime}})\,.

(24)

The mode functions
\xi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})
and
\chi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})
are associated with definite helicity. We decompose them in terms of the unit helicity eigenspinors
h_{s}
as

\xi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})=u_{s}(\tau,k)\hskip 0.85358pth_{s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,\qquad\chi^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}}_{s}(\tau,\mathbf{k})=v_{s}(\tau,k)\hskip 0.85358pts\hskip 0.85358pth^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}}_{-s}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt.

(25)

The helicity basis eigenspinors
h_{s}
satisfy

\vec{\sigma}\hskip-0.85358pt\cdot\!\hat{\,\bf k}\,h_{s}(\mathbf{k})=s\,h_{s}(\mathbf{k}),\qquad h_{s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})h_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})=\delta_{ss^{\prime}},\qquad\sum_{s=\pm 1}\!\!h_{s}(\mathbf{k})h_{s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})=1.

(26)

For the spatial momentum along the direction

\hat{\,\mathbf{k}}=(\sin\theta\cos\varphi,\hskip 0.85358pt\sin\theta\sin\varphi,\hskip 0.85358pt\cos\theta)\hskip 0.85358pt,

(27)

the helicity eigenspinors can be chosen as follows:

h_{+}(\theta,\varphi)=\!\begin{pmatrix}\!e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\cos\!\frac{\theta}{2}\\[2.84526pt]
\!e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\varphi}{2}}\sin\!\frac{\theta}{2}\end{pmatrix}\!,\quad~~h_{-}(\theta,\varphi)=\!\begin{pmatrix}\!-e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\sin\!\frac{\theta}{2}\\
e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\cos\!\frac{\theta}{2}\end{pmatrix}\!.

(28)

More details about these helicity eigenspinors
and the derivation of their relations are given in the Appendix
A
.

Then, from the equations of motion we derive the equations for the
\tau
-dependent
spinor coefficients
u_{s}
and
v_{s}

(with
s\hskip-0.85358pt=\hskip-0.85358pt\pm
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptu_{\pm}^{\prime}\pm ku_{\pm}

\displaystyle=a\lambda u_{\pm}\!+\hskip-0.85358ptaM_{\!R}v_{\pm}\,,

(29a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptv_{\pm}^{\prime}\mp kv_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-a\lambda v_{\pm}\!+\hskip-0.85358ptaM_{\!R}u_{\pm}\,,

(29b)

where
u^{\prime}\!=\!\text{d}u/\text{d}\tau
and

v^{\prime}\!=\!\text{d}v/\text{d}\tau
. To solve the above mixed differential equations,
we recast them in the following second-order differential equations on
u_{\pm}
and
v_{\pm}
respectively,

\displaystyle u_{\pm}^{\prime\prime}\!-\hskip-0.85358ptaHu_{\pm}^{\prime}\!+\!\Big[\hskip-0.85358pt(\pm k\!-\hskip-0.85358pta\lambda)^{2}\!+\hskip-0.85358pta^{2}M_{\!R}^{2}\!\pm\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaHk\Big]u_{\pm}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(30a)

\displaystyle v_{\pm}^{\prime\prime}\!-\hskip-0.85358ptaHv_{\pm}^{\prime}\!+\!\Big[\hskip-0.85358pt(\mp k\!+\hskip-0.85358pta\lambda)^{2}\!+\hskip-0.85358pta^{2}M_{\!R}^{2}\!\mp\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaHk\Big]v_{\pm}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(30b)

Imposing the Bunch-Davies initial condition
(for
\tau\!\rightarrow\!-\infty
)
together with the canonical normalization,
the spinor coefficients
u_{s}
and
v_{s}
are solved
as follows:

\displaystyle u_{+}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{+\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}}{\sqrt{-2k\tau\,}\,}\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358ptW_{\!-\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda},\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2k\tau)\,,

(31)

\displaystyle u_{-}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}}{\sqrt{-2k\tau\,}\,}W_{\!\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda},\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2k\tau)\,,

\displaystyle v_{+}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{+\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}}{\sqrt{-2k\tau\,}\,}W_{\!\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda},\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2k\tau)\,,

\displaystyle v_{-}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}}{\sqrt{-2k\tau\,}\,}\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358ptW_{\!-\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda},\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2k\tau)\,,

where
W_{\kappa,\mu}(z)
denotes the Whittaker function, and

\tilde{M}_{\!R}=\frac{M_{\!R}}{\hskip 0.85358ptH\,}\hskip 0.85358pt,\quad~~\tilde{\lambda}=\frac{\lambda}{\hskip 0.85358ptH\,}\hskip 0.85358pt,\quad~\lambda=\frac{\,\dot{\phi}_{0}\,}{\Lambda}\hskip 0.85358pt,\quad~\tilde{\nu}=\!\sqrt{\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}\!+\!\tilde{\lambda}^{2}\,}\,.

(32)

On the other hand, the Whittaker function
W_{\kappa,\mu}(z)
can be expressed in terms of the Kummer’s confluent hypergeometric function
{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}(a;b;z)
as follows:

W_{\kappa,\mu}(z)=\frac{\Gamma(-2\mu)\hskip 0.85358pte^{-z/2}z^{\mu+\frac{1}{2}}\,}{\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}\!-\!\mu\!-\!\kappa)\,}{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}\!\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\!+\!\mu\!-\!\kappa;1\!+\!2\mu;z\right)\!+\hskip-0.85358pt(\mu\!\!\rightarrow\!\!-\mu)\,,

(33)

where the function
{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}(a;b;z)

is defined as

{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}(a;b;z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\!\frac{~(a)_{n}z^{n}~}{~(b)_{n}\hskip 0.85358ptn!~}\,,

(34)

with
(a)_{n}
being the rising Pochhammer symbol,

(a)_{n}\equiv\frac{\,\Gamma(a\!+\!n)\,}{\Gamma(a)}\,.

(35)

Accordingly, the spinor coefficients
u_{s}
and
v_{s}
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptu_{+}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4+\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}\tilde{M}_{\!R}e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\,}{\Gamma(1\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}\!-\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}(-2k\tau)^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}e^{-\mathrm{i}k\tau}{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu};1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu};\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2k\tau)\!+\!(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptu_{-}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4-\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})}{\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu})}(-2k\tau)^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}e^{-\mathrm{i}k\tau}{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}(-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}+\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu};1+\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu};\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2k\tau)+(\tilde{\nu}\rightarrow-\tilde{\nu})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptv_{+}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4+\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})}{\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu})}(-2k\tau)^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}e^{-\mathrm{i}k\tau}{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}(\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}+\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu};1+\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu};\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2k\tau)+(\tilde{\nu}\rightarrow-\tilde{\nu})\,,

(36)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptv_{-}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4-\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}\tilde{M}_{\!R}e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})}{\Gamma(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}(-2k\tau)^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}e^{-\mathrm{i}k\tau}{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu};1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu};\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2k\tau)\!+\!(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\,.

Note that the Whittaker function has a branch cut in the
z
-plane. As a result, performing a Wick rotation in the calculation of the correlation function is not entirely straightforward. In contrast, the function
{}_{1}\mathrm{F}_{1}(a;b;z)
is regular in the
z
-plane, which makes the Wick rotation simpler and facilitates the evaluation of the desired correlation function.

To extract the oscillatory CC signals, it is useful to determine the late-time behavior of these spinor coefficients. In the soft limit or late time limit
|k\tau|\!\rightarrow\!0\hskip 0.85358pt
, their asymptotic forms are given by

\displaystyle u_{\text{L}+}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle\simeq e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4+\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}\frac{~\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})~}{\,\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}(-2k\tau)^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\!+\!(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\,,

(37)

\displaystyle u_{\text{L}-}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle\simeq e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4-\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}\frac{e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})}{\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu})}(-2k\tau)^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}+(\tilde{\nu}\rightarrow-\tilde{\nu}),

\displaystyle v_{\text{L}+}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle\simeq e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4+\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}\frac{e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})}{\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu})}(-2k\tau)^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}+(\tilde{\nu}\rightarrow-\tilde{\nu}),

\displaystyle v_{\text{L}-}(\tau,k)

\displaystyle\simeq e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4-\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}\tilde{M}_{\!R}\frac{e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})}{\Gamma(1-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu})}(-2k\tau)^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}+(\tilde{\nu}\rightarrow-\tilde{\nu})\,,

with the
\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{L}\pm}(\tau,k)\equiv\lim_{|k\tau|\rightarrow 0}\!u_{\pm}(\tau,k)
and

\displaystyle v_{\mathrm{L}\pm}(\tau,k)\equiv\lim_{|k\tau|\rightarrow 0}\!v_{\pm}(\tau,k)
.

In the next section, we will introduce the propagators of Weyl spinor,

D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
,
which are constructed from products of
two spinor coefficients. The late time limit of a “propagator” contributes to the CC signal. For instance, for the propagator
D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\simeq u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u^{\dagger}_{+}(\tau,k)
, it has the late time limit,

\displaystyle u_{\text{L}+}(\tau_{1},k)u^{\dagger}_{\text{L}+}(\tau_{2},k)

\displaystyle=\frac{-e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{~\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})~}(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}+(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})

(38)

\displaystyle+\frac{-e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}+\pi\tilde{\nu}}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}\hskip 0.85358pt\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})}{~\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})~}\!\!\left(\!\frac{\tau_{1}}{\,\tau_{2}\,}\!\right)^{\!\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\!\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})

\displaystyle=\left[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!+\!\left[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\mathrm{(Loc)}}.

Similar to the case of the scalar field, the late time limit of a fermion propagator
could also be divided into the nonlocal part proportional to
(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}

(accompanied by a non-analytic dependence on
k
) and the local part proportional to

\left(\!\frac{\,\tau_{1}\,}{\,\tau_{2}\,}\!\right)^{\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}
. In this work, we are interested in the nonlocal part of the cosmological collider signal
generated by the right-handed neutrino, so we extract the nonlocal contribution
by retaining only the terms proportional to

(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}
and
(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}
. For instance, for the product

u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)
,
the nonlocal part (denoted by the abbreviation “NLoc”) is given by

\displaystyle\left[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{~\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})~}(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\!+\!(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})

(39)

\displaystyle=\sum_{d=\pm}\!\left[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)},d},

where
\Big[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\Big]_{\mathrm{(NLoc)},d}
denotes the component proportional to

(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}
,
with
d\!=\!\pm\hskip 0.85358pt
. In the following, we summarize the nonlocal parts for all relevant products. As shown above, we obtain the nonlocal contribution, denoted by “
(NLoc)
”, by summing over the two components “
\mathrm{(NLoc)},d
” with
d=\pm\hskip 0.85358pt
,

\displaystyle\left[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{-e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2d\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{~\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})~}(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu}},

(40a)

\displaystyle\left[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)v_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2d\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{~\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})~}(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu}},

(40b)

\displaystyle\left[v_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2d\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{~\Gamma(1-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})~}(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu}},

(40c)

\displaystyle\left[u_{-}(\tau_{1},k)u_{-}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{e^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2d\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{~\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})~}(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu}},

(40d)

\displaystyle\left[u_{-}(\tau_{1},k)v_{-}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\mathrm{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2d\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{~\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})~}(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu}},

(40e)

\displaystyle\left[v_{-}(\tau_{1},k)u_{-}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\mathrm{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2d\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{~\Gamma(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu})~}(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptd\tilde{\nu}}.

(40f)

Using the properties of the nonlocal part,
we can express the remaining cases
in terms of the above results
(for
s\!=\!\pm
),

\displaystyle\left[v_{s}(\tau_{1},k)v_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=-\left[u_{s}(\tau_{1},k)u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d},

(41a)

\displaystyle\left[v_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{1},k)u_{s}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=\left[u_{s}(\tau_{1},k)v_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d},

(41b)

\displaystyle\left[u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{1},k)v_{s}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d}

\displaystyle=\left[v_{s}(\tau_{1},k)u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)},d}.

(41c)

2.3
Fermion Propagators during Inflation in the SK Formalism

The Schwinger–Keldysh (SK) path integral formalism is used to compute expectation values of operators at a finite time in a time-dependent background, as required for deriving cosmological correlators. It evolves the quantum state along a closed time contour, going forward and then backward in time, which ensures a consistent treatment of real-time dynamics. In this framework, fields are doubled into forward
(\oplus)
and backward
(\ominus)
branches, and correlators are obtained from a generating functional defined on this contour. This method naturally incorporates interactions and initial conditions, and is commonly used to compute inflationary correlation functions and primordial non-Gaussianities
21
;
23
;
37
. To compute the inflationary correlators of
\delta\phi(t,\mathbf{x})
generated by the right-handed-neutrino loop, we first present the propagators for Weyl spinors in the SK formalism.

As in flat spacetime, there are two types of propagators for Majorana fermion in term of Weyl spinors. We begin with the propagators of the
\langle\tilde{N}\tilde{N}^{\dagger}\rangle
type, denoted by
D_{ab\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}\hskip 0.85358pt
,
where the SK indices
(a,b)
take the values
\oplus,\ominus
and have four combinations

(\oplus\oplus,\oplus\ominus,\ominus\oplus,\ominus\ominus)
corresponding to the four possible SK contour assignments
and related by time ordering. They are expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07164pt{D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}\!\xi_{s,\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{k})\,\xi^{\dagger}_{s,\dot{\beta}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{k})=\sum_{s}\!u_{s}(\tau_{1},k)\,u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\,h_{s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})h_{s,\dot{\beta}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07164pt{D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}\!-\chi^{\dagger}_{s,\dot{\beta}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{k})\,\chi_{s,\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{k})=\sum_{s}\!-v_{s}(\tau_{2},k)\,v_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{1},k)\,h^{\dagger}_{-s,\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k})h_{-s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07164pt{D_{\oplus\oplus}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle={D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt{D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\hskip-0.85358pt\tau_{1})\,,

(42)

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07164pt{D_{\ominus\ominus}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle={D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\hskip-0.85358pt\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt{D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\hskip-0.85358pt\tau_{1})\,,

where
\theta(\tau)
is the step function,

\theta(\tau)=\left\{\begin{matrix}0\hskip 0.85358pt,&~~~~(\tau<0)\hskip 0.85358pt,\\[2.84526pt]
1\hskip 0.85358pt,&~~~~(\tau\geqslant 0)\hskip 0.85358pt,\end{matrix}\right.

(43)

and
\sum_{s}
denotes the sum over
the two helicity states (
s\hskip-0.85358pt=\hskip-0.85358pt\pm)
.

We call these four propagators the “type-1” propagators and give their diagrammatic representation as follows:

\raisebox{-25.60747pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{diagram/fermion1arrow.pdf}}}\hskip-11.38109pt\equiv\,{D_{ab}}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,.

(44)

In addition, there are four propagators of the
\langle\tilde{N}\tilde{N}\rangle
type, denoted by
\hat{D}_{ab\,\alpha\beta}
,
which are expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07164pt\hat{D}_{\ominus\oplus\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}\!\xi_{s,\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{k})\chi_{s,\beta}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{k})=\sum_{s}\!s\,u_{s}(\tau_{1},k)v_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)h_{s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})h_{-s,\beta}(\mathbf{k})\,,

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{\oplus\ominus\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}\!-\xi_{s,\beta}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{k})\chi_{s,\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{k})=\sum_{s}\!-s\,u_{s}(\tau_{2},k)v_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{1},k)h_{s,\beta}(\mathbf{k})h_{-s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\,,

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{\oplus\oplus\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\hat{D}_{\ominus\oplus\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\hat{D}_{\oplus\ominus\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1})\,,

(45)

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{\ominus\ominus\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\hat{D}_{\oplus\ominus\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\hat{D}_{\ominus\oplus\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1})\,.

We refer to these four propagators as the “type-2” propagators. Their diagrammatic representation is

\raisebox{-25.60747pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{diagram/fermion2arrow.pdf}}}\hskip-11.38109pt\equiv\hat{D}_{ab\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,.

(46)

The two oppositely oriented arrows indicate that this propagator connects two identical Weyl fields,
and thus represents a Majorana contraction rather than an
N
-number-conserving propagator.

The indices can be raised by contracting with the antisymmetric tensor
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}
:

\hat{D}_{ab\alpha}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\beta}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\equiv\epsilon^{\beta\gamma}\hat{D}_{ab\alpha\gamma}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=-\hat{D}_{ab\alpha\gamma}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\epsilon^{\gamma\beta}.

(47)

Moreover, the Hermitian conjugates of the above propagators, namely those of the
\langle\tilde{N}^{\dagger}\tilde{N}^{\dagger}\rangle
type, are denoted by
\check{D}_{ab\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\hskip 0.85358pt
. For clarity, we refer to them as the “type-3” propagators and express them as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\check{D}_{\ominus\oplus\,\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}\!\chi^{\dagger}_{s,\dot{\alpha}}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{k})\xi^{\dagger}_{s,\dot{\beta}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{k})=\sum_{s}\!s\,v_{s}(\tau_{1},k)u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)h_{-s,\dot{\alpha}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})h_{s,\dot{\beta}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\check{D}_{\oplus\ominus\,\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}\!-\chi^{\dagger}_{s,\dot{\beta}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{k})\xi^{\dagger}_{s,\dot{\alpha}}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{k})=\sum_{s}\!-s\,v_{s}(\tau_{2},k)u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{1},k)h_{-s,\dot{\beta}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})h_{s,\dot{\alpha}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\check{D}_{\oplus\oplus\,\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\check{D}_{\ominus\oplus\,\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\check{D}_{\oplus\ominus\,\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1})\,,

(48)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\check{D}_{\ominus\ominus\,\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\check{D}_{\oplus\ominus\,\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\check{D}_{\ominus\oplus\,\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1})\,.

The dotted spinor indices can be raised
likewise by contracting with the antisymmetric tensor
\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}
:

\check{D}_{ab\,\,\,\dot{\beta}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\dot{\alpha}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\equiv\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\gamma}}\check{D}_{ab\dot{\gamma}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=-\epsilon^{\dot{\gamma}\dot{\alpha}}\check{D}_{ab\dot{\gamma}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,.

(49)

Their diagrammatic representation is given by

\raisebox{-25.60747pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{diagram/fermion3arrow.pdf}}}\hskip-11.38109pt\equiv\check{D}_{ab\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,.

(50)

As discussed in Section
D
, the imprint of the cosmological collider signals can be read off directly from the late-time limit of the massive propagators. For instance, we can extract the nonlocal parts of the spinor propagators in the
late-time limit:

\displaystyle\left[D_{\ominus\oplus\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\left[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!h_{+,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})h_{+,{\dot{\beta}}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})+\left[u_{-}(\tau_{1},k)u_{-}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!h_{-,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})h_{-,{\dot{\beta}}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\left\{\!\left[\frac{-e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{\,\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})h_{+}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})+\frac{e^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{\,\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}h_{-}(\mathbf{k})h_{-}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\!\right]\right.

(51a)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\biggr\}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\!+(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\,,

\displaystyle\left[\hat{D}_{\ominus\oplus\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\left[u_{+}(\tau_{1},k)v_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!h_{+,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})h_{-,\beta}(\mathbf{k})-\!\left[u_{-}(\tau_{1},k)v_{-}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!h_{-,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})h_{+,\beta}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\left\{\!\left[\frac{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}h_{+}(\mathbf{k})h_{-}(\mathbf{k})\!-\!\frac{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}h_{-}(\mathbf{k})h_{+}(\mathbf{k})\!\right]\right.

(51b)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\biggr\}_{\alpha\beta}\!+(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\,,

\displaystyle\left[\check{D}_{\ominus\oplus{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\left[v_{+}(\tau_{1},k)u_{+}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!h_{-,{\dot{\alpha}}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})h_{+,{\dot{\beta}}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})-\!\left[v_{-}(\tau_{1},k)u_{-}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!h_{+,{\dot{\alpha}}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})h_{-,{\dot{\beta}}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\left\{\!\left[\frac{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{\Gamma(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}h_{-}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})h_{+}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})-\frac{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}}{\Gamma(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}h_{+}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})h_{-}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\right.\right]

(51c)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times(4k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\biggr\}_{{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}+(\tilde{\nu}\rightarrow-\tilde{\nu})\,,

In the above expressions, the first term in the nonlocal part arises from the positive-helicity spinor coefficients, namely
u_{+}u_{+}^{\dagger}
,
u_{+}v_{+}^{\dagger}
, and
v_{+}u_{+}^{\dagger}
, whereas the second term arises from the negative-helicity spinor coefficients, namely
u_{-}u_{-}^{\dagger}
,
u_{-}v_{-}^{\dagger}
, and
v_{-}u_{-}^{\dagger}
.

Without losing generality,
we choose a positive chemical potential
\lambda\!>\!0\hskip 0.85358pt
. Then, we readily find
3
3

3

For the Gamma functions appearing in the above expressions, one may use the asymptotic formula,
\Gamma(x\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pty)\!\sim\!\sqrt{2\pi\,}|y|^{x-1/2}e^{-\pi|y|/2}
,
in the regime
\lambda\!\gg\!m\!\gg\!H\hskip 0.85358pt
.

that the positive-helicity contribution, namely
u_{+}u_{+}^{\dagger}
,
u_{+}v_{+}^{\dagger}
,
and
v_{+}u_{+}^{\dagger}
, is proportional to the modified Boltzmann factor

e^{+\pi\tilde{\lambda}-\pi\tilde{\nu}}\!\thicksim\!e^{-\pi\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}/2\tilde{\lambda}}
when the chemical potential
\lambda\hskip 0.85358pt
is sufficiently large. In contrast, in the absence of a chemical potential
(
\lambda\!=\!0
), the usual Boltzmann suppression for a heavy fermion is

e^{-\pi\tilde{M}_{\!R}}
. This shows that a positive chemical potential enhances the production of fermions with positive helicity and delays the Boltzmann suppression associated with a large mass
23
;
5
;
4
;
66
;
65
.

In contrast, the negative-helicity contribution, namely
u_{-}u_{-}^{\dagger}
,
u_{-}v_{-}^{\dagger}
, and
v_{-}u_{-}^{\dagger}
, is proportional to
e^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}-\pi\tilde{\nu}}
, indicating that a positive chemical potential suppresses the production of fermions with negative helicity. Similarly, a negative chemical potential would instead enhance the production of fermions with negative helicity. In either case, one helicity component of the Weyl spinor is enhanced in the presence of a sufficiently large chemical potential. Thus, it suffices for us to consider
the case of a positive chemical potential
\lambda\!>\!0\,
.

2.4
Seed Integrals for Fermion Propagators

In Section
3
,
we will evaluate the inflaton two-point correlator
\mathcal{J}_{ab}(\mathbf{k})
containing a single fermion propagator,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{ab}(\mathbf{k})\thicksim\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\text{d}\tau_{2}\,G_{a}(k,\tau_{1})\hskip 0.85358ptD_{ab}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip 0.85358ptG_{b}(k,\tau_{2})\,,

(52)

where
G_{a,b}
is the bulk-to-boundary propagator of the inflaton
\delta\phi
. Such two-point correlator

\mathcal{J}_{ab}(\mathbf{k})

will serve as an ingredient in the evaluation of the three-point inflaton correlator induced by the fermion loop, which can be expressed
by a set of seed integrals. In the following, we introduce these seed integrals and organize them in a
convenient form for the subsequent calculations.

The bulk-to-boundary propagators of the inflaton
\delta\phi
, defined in Eq.(
252
), can always be written as the product of a function of
\tau
and an oscillatory exponential,

G_{a}(k,\tau)=P(\tau)e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptak\tau},\qquad P(\tau)=\sum_{n=0}^{2}\!c(n)\hskip 0.85358pt\tau^{n},

(53)

where
P(\tau)
is a finite polynomial
of the conformal time
\tau
. Thus, substituting

G_{a}(\mathbf{k},\tau)
into
the two-point correlator
\mathcal{J}_{ab}

in Eq.(
52
),
we derive the following seed time integral:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{ab}

\displaystyle=k^{1+p_{1}}k^{1+p_{2}}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\text{d}\tau_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptak\tau_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptbk\tau_{2}}(-\tau_{1})^{p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{p_{2}}D_{ab}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(54)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptbz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}D_{ab}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})\,,

where we have introduced the dimensionless variables

z_{i}=-k\hskip 0.85358pt\tau_{i}\,,\qquad(i=1,2)\hskip 0.85358pt.

(55)

The fermion propagators
D_{ab}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
are constructed from the two types of spinor coefficients,
u_{s}(k,\tau)
and
v_{s}(k,\tau)
, together with their conjugates. As a result, the relevant time integrals reduce to two basic forms. Taking
u_{s}(z_{1})v_{s}^{\dagger}(z_{2})

as an example, the first form is
the factorized time integral,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{s}(z_{1})v_{s}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\,,

(56)

and the second form is the
nested time integral,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaz_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{s}(z_{1})v_{s}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\!\times\!\begin{cases}\,\theta(z_{1}\!-\!z_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt,\\
\,\theta(z_{2}\!-\!z_{1})\hskip 0.85358pt,\end{cases}

(57)

where
a\!=\!\oplus,\ominus\hskip 0.85358pt
,
and
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are the relevant powers associated with
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, respectively.
Hence, there are two basic classes of seed integrals: the factorized integrals in Eq.(
56
) (corresponding to
a\!=\!-b\!=\!\oplus,\ominus
),
and the nested integrals in Eq.(
57
) (corresponding to
a\!=\!b\!=\!\oplus,\ominus
). In what follows, we define the seed integrals for both classes.

All seed integrals introduced below can be evaluated by means of a Wick rotation
68
;
21
;
25
;
26
,

e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}},\,e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;e^{-z_{1}},\,e^{-z_{2}}\,,

(58)

where the rotation directions are chosen such that the integrals remain convergent. The validity of this procedure is ensured by the usual
\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\epsilon
prescription of the interacting quantum field theory
for de Sitter spacetime.

In the following
Subsections
2.4.1
-
2.4.3
,
we first present the seed integrals in the special frame where the spatial momentum is aligned with
z
-axis,

\mathbf{k}=k\hat{z}=(0,0,k)\hskip 0.85358pt.

(59)

Then, in Section
2.4.4
, we will explain how to transform these seed integrals to the general case that has the momentum
\mathbf{k}

in an arbitrary direction.

2.4.1
Seed Integrals for
{D}_{ab\,{\alpha\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})

As discussed above, the seed integrals are classified according to the type of the fermion propagator. We define the seed integrals
\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{ab\hskip 8.53581pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}
associated with the propagator

{D}_{\!ab\,{\alpha\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{ab\hskip 5.69054pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}

\displaystyle=\,k_{1}^{1+p_{1}}k_{2}^{1+p_{2}}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\text{d}\tau_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptak\tau_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptbk\tau_{2}}(-\tau_{1})^{p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{p_{2}}{D}_{ab\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(60)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptbz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}{D}_{ab\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})\,.

Since
{D}_{\!ab\,{\alpha\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})
is a rank-2
spinor-tensor which is constructed by direct product of two spinors, the seed integral
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{ab\hskip 8.53581pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}

could be expressed by a
2\!\times\!2
matrix
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\oplus\oplus\hskip 4.2679pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\!\begin{pmatrix}A_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\!\!-\!B_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}&0\\[2.84526pt]
0&A_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\!-\!B_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\!\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha\dot{\beta}},

(61a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\ominus\ominus\hskip 4.2679pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\!\left(\mathcal{I}_{\oplus\oplus\hskip 4.2679pt\beta\dot{\alpha}}^{p_{2}^{*}p_{1}^{*}}\right)^{\!\dagger}=\!\begin{pmatrix}(A_{1}^{p_{2}^{*}p_{1}^{*}}\!\!-\!B_{2}^{p_{2}^{*}p_{1}^{*}})^{*}&0\\[2.84526pt]
0&(A_{2}^{p_{2}^{*}p_{1}^{*}}\!\!-\!B_{1}^{p_{2}^{*}p_{1}^{*}})^{*}\!\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha\dot{\beta}},

(61b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\ominus\oplus\hskip 4.2679pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\!\begin{pmatrix}U_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}&0\\[2.84526pt]
0&U_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha\dot{\beta}},\hskip 22.76219pt\mathcal{I}_{\oplus\ominus\hskip 4.2679pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=\!\begin{pmatrix}-V_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}&\!\!\!0\\[2.84526pt]
0&\!\!-V_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\!\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha\dot{\beta}}.

(61c)

In Eq.(
61b
), the seed integral

\mathcal{I}_{\ominus\ominus\hskip 4.2679pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
is connected to
\mathcal{I}_{\oplus\oplus\hskip 4.2679pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
by the identity
\mathcal{I}_{\ominus\ominus\hskip 4.2679pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\hskip-0.85358pt\!=\!\hskip-0.85358pt\left(\mathcal{I}_{\oplus\oplus\hskip 4.2679pt\beta\dot{\alpha}}^{p_{2}^{*}p_{1}^{*}}\right)^{\!\dagger}
. This can be proved in the following way:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\ominus\ominus\hskip 4.2679pt\alpha\dot{\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}{D}_{\ominus\ominus\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})

(62)

\displaystyle=\bigg[\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}^{*}}z_{2}^{p_{2}^{*}}\!\Big(\!{D}_{\ominus\ominus\,\alpha\dot{\beta}}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})\!\Big)^{\!\!\dagger}\bigg]^{\!\dagger}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}^{*}}z_{2}^{p_{2}^{*}}{D}_{\oplus\oplus\,\beta\dot{\alpha}}(\mathbf{k},z_{2},z_{1})\bigg]^{\!\dagger}=\left(\mathcal{I}_{\oplus\oplus\hskip 4.2679pt\beta\dot{\alpha}}^{p_{2}^{*}p_{1}^{*}}\right)^{\!\dagger}.

As discussed above, we choose
\mathbf{k}

to be aligned with the
z
-axis, i.e.,
\mathbf{k}\!=\!k\hat{z}
in the seed integral. In Eq.(
61
), the matrix elements
(A_{i}^{p_{1}p_{2}},B_{i}^{p_{1}p_{2}})
(
i\!=\!1,2
)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle A_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{+}(z_{1})u_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\theta(z_{2}\!-\!z_{1})

\displaystyle=\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}I^{p_{1}p_{2}}(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,,

(63a)

\displaystyle A_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{-}(z_{1})u_{-}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\theta(z_{2}\!-\!z_{1})

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}I^{p_{1}p_{2}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,,

(63b)

\displaystyle B_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}v_{+}(z_{2})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{1})\theta(z_{1}\!-\!z_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}I^{p_{2}p_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,,

(63c)

\displaystyle B_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}v_{-}(z_{2})v_{-}^{\dagger}(z_{1})\theta(z_{1}\!-\!z_{2})

\displaystyle=\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}\hskip 0.85358pte^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}I^{p_{2}p_{1}}\hskip-0.85358pt(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,.

(63d)

In the above,
I^{p_{1}p_{2}}(x,y,z)
is given by

\displaystyle I^{p_{1}p_{2}}(x,y,z)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2^{-2-p_{1}-p_{2}}e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi}{2}\left(p_{1}+p_{2}\right)}\pi\,\text{csch}(2\pi\tilde{\nu})\hskip 0.85358pt\Gamma(2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2})

(64)

\displaystyle\times\!\hskip-0.85358pt\Bigg[\frac{\,e^{-\pi\tilde{\nu}}\,}{\,\Gamma(x)\,}\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})

\displaystyle\times{}_{4}\tilde{F}_{3}\!\hskip-0.85358pt\left(\begin{matrix}2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2},\;1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\;y,\;2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\hskip 0.85358pt\\
2\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\;3\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!z,\;1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\end{matrix}\bigg|\!-\!1\!\right)\!-\!(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\Bigg],

where
{}_{4}\tilde{F}_{3}
is the hypergeometric function as defined in Section
1
. The other matrix elements

(U_{i}^{p_{1}p_{2}},V_{i}^{p_{1}p_{2}})
(
i\!=\!1,2
)
are defined by

\displaystyle U_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{+}(z_{1})u_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}K^{p_{1}p_{2}}(2\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda},2\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})\,,

(65a)

\displaystyle U_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{-}(z_{1})u_{-}^{\dagger}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}K^{p_{1}p_{2}}(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda},1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})\,,

(65b)

\displaystyle V_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}v_{+}(z_{2})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{1})

\displaystyle=e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi(p_{1}-p_{2})}K^{p_{1}p_{2}}(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda},1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})\,,

(65c)

\displaystyle V_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}v_{-}(z_{2})v_{-}^{\dagger}(z_{1})

\displaystyle=\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}e^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi(p_{1}-p_{2})}K^{p_{1}p_{2}}(2\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda},2\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})\,,

(65d)

where the function
K^{p_{1}p_{2}}(x,y)

takes the following form,

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptK^{p_{1}p_{2}}(x,y)=\displaystyle e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi}{2}(p_{1}-p_{2})}\frac{~\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})~}{2^{2+p_{1}+p_{2}}\Gamma(p_{1}\!+\!x)\Gamma(p_{2}\!+\!y)}\,.

(66)

The derivations of the above seed-integral matrices are provided in Appendix
C.2
.

2.4.2
Seed Integrals for
\hat{D}_{ab\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})

Then, we consider the propagator
\hat{D}_{ab\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})

and define its associated seed integrals
\hat{\mathcal{I}}_{ab\hskip 8.53581pt\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{I}}_{ab\hskip 5.69054pt\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=k_{1}^{1+p_{1}}k_{2}^{1+p_{2}}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\text{d}\tau_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptak\tau_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptbk\tau_{2}}(-\tau_{1})^{p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{p_{2}}\hat{D}_{ab\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(67)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptbz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}\hat{D}_{ab\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})\,.

The seed integral
\hat{\mathcal{I}}_{ab\hskip 8.53581pt\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
can be expressed as the following
2\!\times\!2
matrix:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{I}}_{\oplus\oplus\hskip 4.2679pt{\alpha\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\!\begin{pmatrix}0&S_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\!+\!S_{2}^{p_{2}p_{1}}\\[2.84526pt]
-\left(S_{1}^{p_{2}p_{1}}\!+\!S_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\right)&0\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha\beta},

(68a)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{I}}_{\ominus\ominus\hskip 4.2679pt{\alpha\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\!\begin{pmatrix}0&S_{3}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\!+\!S_{4}^{p_{2}p_{1}}\\[2.84526pt]
-\left(S_{3}^{p_{2}p_{1}}\!+\!S_{4}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\right)&0\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha\beta},

(68b)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{I}}_{\oplus\ominus\hskip 4.2679pt{\alpha\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\!\begin{pmatrix}0&W_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\\[2.84526pt]
-W_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}&0\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha\beta},\hskip 22.76219pt\hat{\mathcal{I}}_{\ominus\oplus\hskip 4.2679pt{\alpha\beta}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=\!\begin{pmatrix}0&W_{2}^{p_{2}p_{1}}\\[2.84526pt]
-W_{1}^{p_{2}p_{1}}&0\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha\beta}.

(68c)

In the above seed integrals, we define

S_{i}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
(
i=1,2,3,4
) as follows:

\displaystyle S_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{+}(z_{1})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\,\theta(z_{2}\!-\!z_{1})

\displaystyle=\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}I^{p_{1}p_{2}}(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,,

(69a)

\displaystyle S_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{-}(z_{1})v_{-}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\,\theta(z_{2}\!-\!z_{1})

\displaystyle=\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}I^{p_{1}p_{2}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,,

(69b)

\displaystyle S_{3}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{+}(z_{1})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\,\theta(z_{1}\!-\!z_{2})

\displaystyle=\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}J^{p_{2}p_{1}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,,

(69c)

\displaystyle S_{4}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{-}(z_{1})v_{-}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\,\theta(z_{1}\!-\!z_{2})

\displaystyle=\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}J^{p_{2}p_{1}}(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,,

(69d)

where the function
I^{p_{1}p_{2}}(x,y,z)

is defined in Eq.(
64
),
and the function
J^{p_{1}p_{2}}(x,y,z)

takes the following form:

\displaystyle J^{p_{1}p_{2}}(x,y,z)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2^{-2-p_{1}-p_{2}}e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi}{2}\left(p_{1}+p_{2}\right)}\pi\,\text{csch}(2\pi\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2})

(70)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg[\frac{\,e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}}\,}{\,\Gamma(x)\,}\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})

\displaystyle\times{}_{4}\tilde{F}_{3}\!\left(\begin{matrix}2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2},\;1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\;y,\;2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\hskip 0.85358pt\\
2\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\;3\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!z,\;1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\end{matrix}\bigg|-\!\!1\!\right)\!-\!(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\Bigg]\,.

In Eq.(
68
), the matrix
elements
W_{i}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
(
i=1,2
)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle W_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{+}(z_{1})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})=\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}K^{p_{1}p_{2}}(2\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda},1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})\,,

(71a)

\displaystyle W_{2}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{-}(z_{1})v_{-}^{\dagger}(z_{2})=\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{-\pi\tilde{\lambda}}K^{p_{1}p_{2}}(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda},2\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})\,,

(71b)

where the function
K^{p_{1}p_{2}}(x,y)

is defined in Eq.(
66
).

2.4.3
Seed Integrals for
\check{D}_{ab\,{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k},z_{1},z_{2})

Similarly, we consider the propagator
\check{D}_{ab\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
and
define its associated seed integrals
\check{\mathcal{I}}_{ab\hskip 8.53581pt\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{I}}_{ab\hskip 8.53581pt{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=k_{1}^{1+p_{1}}k_{2}^{1+p_{2}}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\text{d}\tau_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptak\tau_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptbk\tau_{2}}(-\tau_{1})^{p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{p_{2}}\check{D}_{ab\,{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(72)
