\displaystyle=\frac{5}{\,72\hskip 0.85358pt\pi^{4}P_{\zeta}^{2}\,}\frac{k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}k_{3}^{2}}{\,\mathcal{C}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\,}\langle\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{3}}\rangle^{\prime}_{\text{squeezed}}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{\,72\hskip 0.85358pt\pi^{4}P_{\zeta}^{2}\,}\!\!\left(\!\!\frac{\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}\,}{\dot{\phi}_{0}}\!\!\right)^{\!\!3}\!\!\frac{k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}k_{3}^{2}}{\,\mathcal{C}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\,}\langle\delta\phi_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}\delta\phi_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}\delta\phi_{\mathbf{k}_{3}}\rangle^{\prime}_{\text{squeezed}}

\displaystyle\simeq\Bigg|\frac{~10\hskip 0.85358pt\pi^{3}e^{2\pi\tilde{\lambda}-5\pi\tilde{\nu}}(\mathrm{i}\!+\!2\tilde{\nu})^{2}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt3\!+\!4\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})S_{3}^{1-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\,1-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\tilde{\lambda}^{3}\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{3}P_{\zeta}~}{\,9\hskip 0.85358pt\Gamma(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})^{2}\Gamma(5\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt4\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}\Bigg|\,,

(134)

where in the above second line we have used

\zeta\!=\!-\frac{H}{\,\dot{\phi}_{0}\,}\delta\phi\,
. The quantity
S_{3}^{1-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu},1-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}
is one of the entries
in the seed integral matrix

\hat{\mathcal{I}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\ominus\ominus\,\alpha\beta}

(with
p_{1}\!=\!p_{2}\!=\!1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}
)
as defined in Eqs.(
68b
) and (
69
),

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692ptS_{3}^{1-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}=

\displaystyle\,2^{-3+\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}}e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}-2\pi\tilde{\nu}}\pi\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{M}_{\!R}\Gamma(2\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})

\displaystyle\times\!\hskip-0.85358pt\Bigg\{\!\hskip-0.85358pt\!-\!\frac{1}{\,(-\mathrm{i}\!+\!\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\tilde{\nu})(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\!-\!\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\tilde{\nu})^{2}\Gamma(2\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 5.69054pt\!+\!\frac{1}{\,(-\mathrm{i}\!+\!\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\tilde{\nu})(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\!+\!\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\,{}_{3}\tilde{F}_{2}\!\Bigg(\begin{matrix}2,\,-2\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\,2\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\,\\
3\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\,-1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\end{matrix}\bigg|\!-\!1\!\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\,\,\,\,\!+\!4(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\!+\!\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\tilde{\nu})(1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,{}_{3}\tilde{F}_{2}\!\Bigg(\begin{matrix}3,\,-1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\,3\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\,\\
4\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\end{matrix}\bigg|\!-\!1\!\Bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\,\,\,\,\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pte^{2\pi\tilde{\nu}}\frac{\,\Gamma(4-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(4-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt4\tilde{\nu})\,}{\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}+\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}{}_{4}\tilde{F}_{3}\!\Bigg(\begin{matrix}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\,2\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu},\,4\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu},\,4\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt4\tilde{\nu}\,\\
1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu},\,3\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu},\,5\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt3\tilde{\nu}\end{matrix}\bigg|\!-\!1\!\Bigg)\!\Bigg\}.

(135)

Figure 5
:
Amplitude of the oscillatory non-Gaussianity
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}
as a function of the
right-handed neutrino mass
M_{\!R}
(in the unit of
H_{\mathrm{inf}}
). The (red, pink, blue, green) curves
correspond to
\Lambda\!=\!(60,\,80,\,100,\,120)\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}
respectively. The regions shaded by the light gray, light orange, and light red colors
represent
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\!<\!0.01
,
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\!>\!0.01
, and
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\!>\!0.1

respectively. Each black dashed curve shows the corresponding analytic result
under the large-chemical-potential limit (
\tilde{\lambda}\!\gg\!1
)
as in Eq.(
136
).

We further simplify the above result for the non-Gaussian amplitude
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}

by taking the large-chemical-potential limit
\tilde{\lambda}\!\gg\!1\hskip 0.85358pt
,
which gives the following expression:

f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\simeq\frac{\,5\hskip 0.85358pt\pi^{\frac{\,5\,}{\,2\,}}\,}{\,2^{8}\hskip 0.85358pt9\sqrt{2\,}\,}P_{\zeta}\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{4}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}^{\!\frac{\,9\,}{\,2\,}}e^{-\frac{\hskip 0.85358pt9\pi\hskip 0.85358pt}{2}(\tilde{\nu}-\tilde{\lambda})}\,.

(136)

This clearly shows that the usual the exponential suppression factor

e^{-\pi m/H_{\mathrm{inf}}}
is softened by the chemical potential

e^{-\frac{\hskip 0.85358pt9\pi\hskip 0.85358pt}{2}(\tilde{\nu}-\tilde{\lambda})}\sim e^{-\frac{\hskip 0.85358pt9\pi\hskip 0.85358pt}{4}(\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}/\tilde{\lambda})}
.

In Fig.
5
, we show the amplitude of the oscillatory non-Gaussianity
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}
as a function of the right-handed neutrino mass
M_{\!R}

for a set of benchmark values of the cutoff scale
\Lambda\hskip 0.85358pt
. The (red, pink, blue, green) curves correspond to

\Lambda\!=\!(60,\,80,\,100,\,120)\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}
respectively,
which in turn give
\lambda\!\simeq\!(60,\,45,\,36,\,30)\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}\hskip 0.85358pt
. The regions shaded by the light gray, light orange, and light red colors
indicate
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\!<\!0.01
,
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\!>\!0.01
,
and
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\!>\!0.1
, respectively. In the same plot, we show analytic results
under the large-chemical-potential limit (
\tilde{\lambda}\!\gg\!1
)
as given by Eq.(
136
),
which are depicted by the black dashed curves for each case.

As shown in Fig.
5
, the oscillatory non-Gaussianity depends sensitively on both the cutoff scale and the right-handed neutrino mass. When the cutoff scale is reduced towards the unitarity bound,
(\dot{\phi}_{0})^{1/2}\!\simeq\!60H_{\mathrm{inf}}
, the cosmological collider signal
can reach

f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\!=\!{O}(0.1)
for

M_{\!R}\!=\!(3\!-\!6)\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}\hskip 0.85358pt
. Such signals may be probed by the future searches through the measurements
of the CMB, the large-scale-structure, and especially the 21cm tomography. Even for a moderately larger cutoff, such as
\Lambda\!=\!80\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}
,
part of the parameter space with
M_{\!R}\!=\!(2\!-\!6)\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}

can still give
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}\!\gtrsim\!{O}(0.01)
,
which may be probed by the future 21cm tomography surveys.
Fig.
5
also shows that the cosmological collider signal
becomes weaker as
\Lambda
increases, or equivalently, as the chemical potential
\lambda\!=\!\dot{\phi}_{0}/\Lambda
decreases. This trend can be understood from the factorized structure discussed in Section
3.2
. As shown in Fig.
4
, the left and right subdiagrams
are both enhanced by a larger chemical potential, and this enhancement
dominates over the bubble contribution (which becomes smaller as the
chemical potential increases). Consequently, the full oscillatory signal becomes stronger when the
chemical potential increases.
This feature highlights the central role of the chemical potential. In the absence of a chemical potential, the cosmological collider signal
from heavy fermions is strongly suppressed by the usual Boltzmann factor,
and an additional suppression arises from the loop level. In contrast, a nonzero chemical potential softens the Boltzmann suppression
and significantly enhances the dominant helicity mode. This opens up
a window for observing the cosmological collider signals
generated by heavy fermions such as the right-handed neutrinos.

Figure
5
also shows that the non-Gaussianity
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}

is suppressed in both the large-mass and small-mass regions. In the large-mass region, the suppression is controlled by the Boltzmann factor. In the small-mass region, the suppression is determined by
two complementary factors. First, certain left subdiagrams in
Fig.
3
, especially the diagrams (3)–(7),
are effectively controlled by mass-insertion-type couplings. As the Majorana mass decreases, the corresponding mass-insertions
become less important, and the oscillatory signals become reduced. Second, the suppression is also encoded in the soft-fermion propagators:
the nonlocal part of the type-1 propagator in Eq.(
2.3
)
scales as
M_{\!R}^{2}
in the small-mass limit, whereas the corresponding
nonlocal parts of the type-2 and type-3 propagators in Eqs.(
2.3
)
and (
2.3
) scale linearly with
M_{\!R}
at the leading order. Combining these factors, we deduce the following:

\lim_{\tilde{M}_{\!R}\rightarrow\hskip 0.85358pt0}\!f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\text{CC}}\sim\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{4}\,,\vskip-5.69054pt

(137)

which shows that the neutrino-loop signal is quickly reduced via
\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{4}

as the heavy fermion mass decreases.

Figure 6
:
Dimensionless shape function
(k_{1}/k_{3})^{2}S(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
plotted as a function of the ratio
k_{1}/k_{3}
on a logarithmic horizontal axis. The (purple-dashed, purple, blue, red, green) curves correspond to

M_{\!R}\!=\!(1,\,2,\,4,\,6)\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}
respectively,
with the chemical potential
\lambda\!=\!60H_{\mathrm{inf}}
. The upper and lower plots correspond to different ranges of
k_{1}/k_{3}
,
illustrating the characteristic logarithmic scaling behavior
along the horizontal axis.

To further characterize the structure of the cosmological collider signal,
one can introduce a dimensionless shape function
S(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

through the following relation:

\langle\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{3}}\rangle^{\prime}=\frac{(2\pi)^{4}P_{\zeta}^{2}}{\,(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3})^{2}\,}S(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\,.

(138)

Unlike the template function
\mathcal{C}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
defined above, the shape function
S(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
also contains the overall amplitude information. Using the squeezed-limit form of the bispectrum, it can be derived as follows:

\displaystyle S(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=\langle\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}\zeta_{\mathbf{k}_{3}}\rangle^{\prime}\frac{\,k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}k_{3}^{2}\,}{\,(2\pi)^{4}P_{\zeta}^{2}~}=\frac{2f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}}{~(2\pi)^{4}P_{\zeta}^{2}~}\!\!\left(\!\!\frac{\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}\,}{\,\dot{\phi}_{0}\,}\!\hskip-0.85358pt\right)^{\hskip-0.85358pt\!\!3}\!\!\left(\!\frac{\,k_{3}\,}{k_{1}}\!\right)^{\hskip-0.85358pt\!\!2}\!\cos\!\hskip-0.85358pt\left(\!\hskip-0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\ln\!\frac{\,k_{1}\,}{\,k_{3}\,}\!+\!\varphi_{0}\!\right)\!.

(139)

In Fig.
6
, we plot the dimensionless quantity

(k_{1}/k_{3})^{2}S(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
as a function of the ratio
k_{1}/k_{3}

on a logarithmic horizontal axis. The (purple-dashed, purple, blue, red, green) curves correspond
to the benchmark masses

M_{\!R}\!=\!(1,\,2,\,4,\,6)\,H_{\mathrm{inf}}

respectively, and the chemical potential takes a sample value

\lambda\!=\!\dot{\phi}_{0}/\Lambda\!=\!60H_{\mathrm{inf}}
.
This figure demonstrates the characteristic oscillatory behavior of
the cosmological collider signals in the squeezed limit. Moreover, because the horizontal axis is logarithmic,
the oscillation frequency can be read off directly from the period
of each curve and is determined by

\omega=2\tilde{\nu}=2\sqrt{\tilde{M}_{\!R}^{2}\!+\!\tilde{\lambda}^{2}\,}\,.

(140)

Hence, the shape of the cosmological collider signal directly
encodes the right-handed-neutrino mass scale and the chemical potential.

Finally, we note that once the chemical potential is switched off, namely in the limit

\tilde{\lambda}\!\rightarrow\!0\hskip 0.85358pt
, the nonlocal part of the symmetric propagator
\left[D^{\mathrm{(S)}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\mathrm{NLoc}}

vanishes. This indicates that the cosmological collider signal generated by a fermion loop
is suppressed in the absence of the chemical potential. Hence, for the case where the heavy fermions couple to the inflaton only through an interaction
that does not induce an effective chemical potential, such as a Yukawa-type coupling,
the resultant cosmological collider signal is expected to be much weaker.

4.2
Comparison with the Previous Work

In this subsection, we briefly compare our result for the inflaton bispectrum with the estimate presented in Ref.
23
, which gave qualitative estimate on the characteristic oscillatory signal generated by heavy fermions during inflation. In contrast, our work uses
a new method to quantitatively compute the cosmological collider signals generated by the
heavy right-handed neutrino loop. In the following, we will explain why the overall amplitude
derived in the present work is numerically smaller than Ref.
23
, and clarify
how this difference arises in our calculation.

We note that the previous analysis
23
adopted two simplifying approximations:
(i)
the hard propagator was treated by a saddle-point approximation;
and
(ii)
the loop momentum was replaced by a characteristic value instead of being integrated over explicitly. By contrast, our calculation is based on the factorized loop treatment developed in Section
3
and Appendix
C
,
in which the diagram is decomposed into a left part, a right part,
and a bubble loop part in which the loop-momentum integration is explicitly performed
within this framework.

Figure 7
:
Comparison for magnitude of the left subdiagrams of the diagrams (1)+(2) in Fig.
2
. The solid curves present the precise result of the left subdiagrams from the diagrams-(1)+(2),
and the dashed curves present the result of the same left subdiagrams using the saddle point approximation adopted by Refs.
23
;
37
;
8
.

The numerical difference between our analysis and the previous one
23

mainly arises from the following sources. First, the propagator structure of Ref.
23
improperly
included all four helicity combinations (
++,\,+-,\,-+,\,--
),
whereas in the present formalism only two independent helicity contributions
(
++,\,--
) enter the final result. This is because the anti-commutator (
24
) vanishes
for the mixed helicity-combinations (
+-,\,-+
). Second, Ref.
23
adopted the saddle-point approximation for
the hard propagator and included the extra helicity channels (
+-,\,-+
)
[which should not exist due to the vanishing anti-commutator
(
24
)]. These two factors enlarged the result of Ref.
23
as compared with
our result, and caused a large discrepancy of order
{O}(10\!-\!100)

in the intermediate-mass range. In contrast, if one keeps only the two helicity channels (
++,\,--
), the saddle-point-approximation-based result for the left subdiagram remains close to the precise result for intermediate mass range, but can deviate by about two orders of magnitude in the large-mass range, as illustrated in Fig.
7
. Third, and more importantly, the loop integration measure is treated very
differently. Ref.
23
effectively approximates the loop integral
as the following over-simplified form,

\frac{1}{\,(2\pi)^{3}\,}\!\!\int\!\mathrm{d}\phi=\frac{1}{\,(2\pi)^{2}\,},

(141)

whereas in the present analysis we compute the full bubble-loop integral explicitly,
as given in Appendix
C.4
. Numerically, this leads to an
O(10^{4})
difference in the intermediate- and large-mass ranges,
as shown in Fig.
9
, and constitutes the dominant source of our new improvements. This main numerical improvement can be traced to the following factor of Gamma functions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\nu})\equiv\frac{~\Gamma\hskip-0.85358pt\big(\!-\!\frac{3}{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}\big)\!\left[\Gamma\big(\frac{3}{2}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}\big)\right]^{\hskip-0.85358pt2}~}{\left[\Gamma(\hskip-0.85358pt-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\right]^{2}\hskip-0.85358pt\Gamma(3\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}\,.

(142)

This factor arises from the explicit loop-momentum integration and is not captured
by the estimate based on pure dimensional analysis of the bubble integral. After removing the phase, it exhibits the large-
\tilde{\nu}
behavior,

|\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\nu})|\!\simeq\!2^{-9/2}\tilde{\nu}^{-3/2}
.
For a sample input of
\tilde{\nu}=30

(corresponding to
\Lambda\!=\!120H_{\mathrm{inf}}
), this factor is of the order

3\!\times\!10^{-4}
. Thus, without including this factor would cause the resultant loop-amplitude
to be over-estimated by about three to four orders of magnitude.

Figure 8
:
Comparison of the correlation amplitude of the cosmological-collider non-Gaussianity
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}

as a function of the heavy-neutrino mass scale
M_{\!R}
,
which is contributed by all diagrams of Fig.
2

as shown in the present plot (a) and contributed by the
diagrams (1)+(2) only as shown in the present plot (b). The (red, pink, blue, green) curves correspond to a set of
sample values of the cutoff scale

\Lambda\!=\!(60,\,80,\,100,\,120)H_{\mathrm{inf}}\hskip 0.85358pt
, respectively.

In addition, Ref.
23
includes only the diagrams (1) and (2)
of Fig.
2
, whereas our calculation includes the complete set of
all eight diagrams of Fig.
2
. Besides, Ref.
23
uses
D_{\ominus\oplus}
as a representative
soft propagator, whereas our calculation employs the symmetric combination
discussed around Eq.(
222
) of
Appendix
C.3
. These differences do not modify the qualitative oscillatory structure of
the signal, but they do affect the overall magnitude by

O(1)
factors.

To make an explicit comparison, we show in Fig.
8

the non-Gaussian amplitude
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}
obtained in our framework
when only diagrams (1) and (2) of Fig.
2
are retained. Even within this special case, the resultant non-Gaussianity
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}

[shown in the present Fig.
8
(a)] is already substantially
smaller than the previous qualitative estimate of Ref.
23

and than our full result [shown in the present Fig.
8
(b)].

We can make more direct comparison in Fig.
9
for the same cutoff
choice
\Lambda\!=\!120H_{\mathrm{inf}}
and for including only
the diagrams (1) and (2) of Fig.
2
. In Fig.
9
, we plot the previous result of
Ref.
23
(shown as blue curve), the result obtained after
correcting the propagator treatment (i.e., the correct helicity contributions
and the precise evaluation of the hard propagator in the left subdiagram)
(shown as green curve), and the full result
obtained in the present work (shown as red curve). As shown in Fig.
9
, the previous result of Ref.
23

(the blue curve) is about one order of magnitude larger than the result
obtained after correcting the propagators (the green curve). The propagator-corrected result (the green curve) is about four orders of magnitude larger than the present result (the red curve). This substantial difference between the green curve and the red curve
is mainly due to the different treatments
of the loop integration measure as given by our formula in
Eq.(
116
) and by the previous approximation
in Eq.(
141
).

Figure 9
:
Comparison among the previous result of Ref.
23

(shown as blue curve),
the revised previous result after correcting the propagators
(shown as green curve), and the new result (shown as red curve),
for a sample cutoff value
\Lambda\!=\!120H_{\mathrm{inf}}
, where only
the diagrams (1) and (2) of Fig.
2
are included
for this comparison.

Figure 10
:
Comparison of the correlation amplitude of the cosmological-collider non-Gaussianity

f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}
between our new result and the estimate of Ref.
23
. The (red, pink, blue, green) solid curves denote our results for

\Lambda\!=\!(60,\,80,\,100,\,120)H_{\mathrm{inf}}
respectively,
whereas the dashed curves denote the corresponding estimates
obtained using the method of Ref.
23
.

Finally, we present a comparison of the correlation amplitude of the cosmological-collider non-Gaussianity

f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}
between our new result (
134
)
and the estimate of Ref.
23

in Fig.
10
,
as a function of the heavy-neutrino mass scale
M_{\!R}
. For the cutoff scales

\Lambda=(60,\,80,\,100,\,120)H_{\mathrm{inf}}
, the previous estimate is larger
than our result by roughly
O(10^{3})
in the intermediate and large mass ranges. This difference is mainly caused by the combined effects of the corrected
fermion propagators, the complete helicity and Schwinger-Keldysh structure,
the inclusion of the full set of relevant loop diagrams, and the explicit
loop-momentum integration performed in the present calculation.

In summary, the above comparison shows that our present method is more precise
and reliable, which leads to substantial improvements of our results over
the previous results
23
. The main difference lies in the overall magnitude of the CC signals,
which is improperly enlarged in Ref.
23
by the
oversimplified approximations described above. In our present approach, the loop integration is consistently factorized
in the squeezed limit, and the full set of relevant diagrams in Fig.
2

is computed for our analyses. We have presented the comparisons
in Fig.
7
, Fig.
8
, Fig.
9
and Fig.
10
. Due to these reasons, our present method and analysis
have made important progress for more reliably predicting
cosmological collider signals from the heavy fermion loop contributions.

5
Conclusions

Cosmological collider physics
24
;
25
;
26
;
14
;
53
;
51
;
33
;
10
;
19
;
40
;
22
;
38
;
39

provides a unique means to probing heavy particles present during inflation
through their contributions to the primordial non-Gaussianities. So far most of the existing studies
have focused on tree-level signals or scalar-loop effects, whereas signatures of heavy fermion loops are
technically more challenging and remain largely unexplored. But, heavy fermions (such as the
right-handed neutrinos) are strongly motivated
76
;
35

from the structure of the Standard Model of particle physics
and from the light neutrino mass-generations through the canonical seesaw
mechanism
48
;
31
;
75
;
32
. Hence, it is important to develop a systematic method for computing the contributions of heavy fermion loops
to the inflaton correlators and to predict the fermion-loop signatures through the primordial non-Gaussianities.

In this work, we studied cosmological collider signals generated by heavy fermion loops in an inflationary setup
including the derivative interaction between the inflaton and right-handed neutrinos. A central ingredient of our analysis is the effective chemical potential induced by the slow-roll inflaton background. This chemical potential leads to helicity-dependent fermion production and can substantially enhance one of the helicity modes,
thereby weakening the usual Boltzmann suppression of heavy-particle production during inflation. In this way, heavy fermions (such as the heavy right-handed neutrinos) that would otherwise generate extremely small signals
may become significantly more visible in the primordial non-Gaussianity.

In Section
2
, we formulated the dynamics of the right-handed Majorana neutrinos in the slow-roll inflationary background. With the unique derivative interaction between the inflaton and right-handed neutrinos as given in Eq.(
13
),
we showed how the rolling inflaton background induces an effective chemical potential (
20
)
for the right-handed neutrinos. We then derived the corresponding Weyl-spinor mode functions (
31
), constructed the Schwinger-Keldysh propagators
in Eqs.(
2.3
), (
2.3
), and (
2.3
), and extracted their late-time nonlocal components
in Eqs.(
51
)–(
51
). These late-time components encode the characteristic oscillatory clock signals and provide
the basic building blocks for the fermion-loop calculation.

In Section
3
, we computed the three-point inflaton correlator generated by the right-handed-neutrino triangle loop. We classified the eight inequivalent contractions of the Majorana fermion loop
[shown in Fig.
2
and Eqs.(
82
)(
3.1
)]
and organized their contributions in terms of the factorized left subdiagram, central bubble loop, and right subdiagram
[shown in Fig.
3
and Eq.(
90
)]. Since the nonlocal parts of fermion propagators contain nontrivial helicity and spinor structures, the scalar-loop cutting rule
cannot be directly applied to the full fermion loop. Hence, we isolated the factorizable nonlocal contribution associated with
the symmetric part of the soft propagators. Within this sector, the loop calculation can be reduced to a controlled product of
three contributions (the left subdiagram, the central bubble loop-integral, and the right subdiagram). This treatment keeps the hard propagator analytically in the left subdiagram and retains the loop-momentum integration
in the central bubble part, thereby improving the control over the normalization of the nonlocal signals as compared with
the previous estimates based on a saddle-point treatment of the hard internal propagator.

In Section
4
, we analyzed the size and shape of the predicted cosmological collider signatures. We expressed the squeezed-limit bispectrum in terms of the oscillatory non-Gaussianity amplitude
f_{\mathrm{NL}}^{\mathrm{CC}}

in Eq.(
134
)
and studied its dependence on the right-handed-neutrino mass, the induced chemical potential, and the
UV cutoff scale of the inflaton-right-handed-neutrino interaction. We found that the chemical potential enhances one of the helicity modes and can substantially soften
the usual Boltzmann suppression of heavy-fermion production. In consequence, as shown Fig.
5
,
the loop-induced signals can become sizable in the intermediate-mass range,
whereas it is suppressed both in the large-mass range by the heavy-particle Boltzmann factor
and in the small-mass range by the mass-dependence of the fermion propagators and the mass-insertion structure. We further compared our result with previous estimates as presented in Figs.
7
-
9
. The main difference arises from a more precise treatment of the full loop structure as shown in Fig.
9
. In earlier analysis, the hard propagator was estimated by a saddle-point approximation and
the magnitude of loop momentum was assumed to be a cutoff value. Moreover, not all fermion-loop contractions were included,
so part of the loop-diagram contributions was effectively omitted. In contrast, we keep the hard propagator inside the left subdiagram,
include the complete set of relevant fermion-loop contractions,
and perform the loop-momentum integration precisely in the bubble subdiagram. This treatment precisely retains the momentum dependence, helicity dependence, and Schwinger-Keldysh structure
of the fermion propagators. In consequence, we found that the resultant signal amplitude is substantially smaller than
the previous estimate, showing that the saddle-point approximation, the oversimplification of loop integral,
and incomplete diagrams cause large overestimate of the loop-induced cosmological collider signals.

Our analysis provides a systematic framework for fermion-loop signals of the cosmological collider physics. We have shown that heavy-fermion derivative interaction with the inflaton can generate potentially observable
oscillatory non-Gaussian signatures even when their masses are significantly above the Hubble scale. This opens up a new window to sensitively probing the high-scale new physics involving heavy fermions,
such as the right-handed neutrinos responsible for the light neutrino mass generation through the seesaw mechanism. In such cosmological collider signals, the oscillation frequency is mainly controlled by
the effective chemical potential, which is set by the cutoff scale
\Lambda
, whereas the signal amplitude
is sensitive to the mass scale of the right-handed neutrino.

We also stress the complementarity between the present study of probing the neutrino seesaw scale
using cosmological collider signals
and the previous study
76
;
35
of probing the neutrino seesaw mechanism
using the local non-Gaussianity. In Refs.
76
;
35
, the local non-Gaussian signals arise from
the Higgs-modulated reheating and depend on the seesaw parameters
(including the right-handed-neutrino mass and the neutrino Yukawa coupling) and
the Higgs self-coupling during inflation. Hence, the two types of observables can probe different combinations of the seesaw and inflationary parameters:
the non-analytic cosmological-collider signals are primarily sensitive to the heavy right-handed-neutrino mass
and the chemical-potential, whereas the local non-Gaussianity constrains the reheating dynamics and
the seesaw parameters (connected to the light neutrino mass as well). Combining these two types of primordial non-Gaussian signatures will give complementary probes
of the full seesaw parameter space.

Appendix

Appendix A
Weyl Spinors in the Inflationary Background

In this Appendix, we retain the spinor indices explicitly and
set up the spinor-helicity conventions. Then, we derive a self-consistent set
of equations of motion for the Weyl spinor mode-functions.

Consider the curved spacetime with a metric tensor
g_{\mu\nu}\hskip 0.85358pt
,

\text{d}s^{2}=g_{\mu\nu}\text{d}x^{\mu}\text{d}x^{\nu}\,.

(143)

Using the vierbein
e^{a}_{\ \mu}\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\partial\xi^{a}\hskip-1.42262pt\big/\partial x^{\mu}
with
\xi^{a}

being the coordinate of the Minkowski metric
\eta_{ab}
,
the metric tensor can be expressed as

g_{\mu\nu}\!=\!e^{a}_{\ \mu}e^{b}_{\ \nu}\eta_{ab}\hskip 0.85358pt
,
where
\eta_{ab}\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)
is the Minkowski metric tensor.

In curved spacetime, spinors are defined with respect to a local Lorentz frame and the covariant derivative includes the spin connection
15
;
52
;
20
. The covariant derivative acting on a Weyl spinor
\psi_{\alpha}
is defined as

\text{D}_{\mu}\psi_{\alpha}=\partial_{\mu}\psi_{\alpha}\!+\!\frac{1}{2}\,\omega_{\mu ab}\big(\Sigma^{ab}\big)_{\!\alpha}^{\,\,\beta}\,\psi_{\beta}\,,

(144)

where
\omega_{\mu ab}
is the spin connection,

\omega_{\mu ab}=e^{a}_{\ \nu}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}e^{b\nu}=e^{a}_{\ \nu}\!\left(\partial_{\mu}e^{b\nu}\!+\!\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\rho}e^{b\rho}\right),

(145)

with
\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\rho}
being the Christoffel symbol. In Eq.(
144
), the quantity

\left(\Sigma^{ab}\right)_{\alpha}^{\,\,\,\,\beta}

denotes the Lorentz generators in the Weyl representation,

\big(\Sigma^{ab}\big)_{\!\alpha}^{\,\,\beta}=\frac{1}{\hskip-1.42271pt4\hskip-1.42271pt}(\sigma^{a}\bar{\sigma}^{b}\!-\!\sigma^{b}\bar{\sigma}^{a})_{\!\alpha}^{\,\,\beta}\,,

(146)

where
\sigma^{a}\!=\!(1,\vec{\sigma})
and

\bar{\sigma}^{a}\!=\!(1,\hskip-0.85358pt-\vec{\sigma})

are the sigma matrices in the flat spacetime.

Then, we consider the spatially flat FLRW metric in the conformal coordinate,

\text{d}s^{2}=a^{2}(\tau)\big(\!-\!\text{d}\tau^{2}\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\text{d}\vec{x}^{\hskip 0.85358pt2}\big).

(147)

The vierbein is given by

e^{a}_{\ \mu}\!=a(\tau)\,\delta^{a}_{\ \mu}

and

e_{a}^{\ \mu}\!=a^{-1}(\tau)\,\delta_{a}^{\ \mu}
. The non-vanishing Christoffel symbols
\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\rho}
are derived as follows:

\Gamma^{0}_{00}\hskip-0.85358pt=\hskip-0.85358pt\mathcal{H}\hskip 0.85358pt,\quad\Gamma^{0}_{ij}\hskip-0.85358pt=\mathcal{H}\hskip 0.85358pt\delta_{ij}\hskip 0.85358pt,\quad\Gamma^{i}_{0j}\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\mathcal{H}\hskip 0.85358pt\delta^{i}_{j}\hskip 0.85358pt,

(148)

where
\mathcal{H}\!=\!\frac{\,a^{\prime}}{a}
with

a^{\prime}\!=\!\text{d}a(\tau)/\text{d}\tau\hskip 0.85358pt
. Thus, the non-vanishing spin connection
components are given by

\omega_{ij0}=-\omega_{i0j}=\mathcal{H}\hskip 0.85358pt\delta_{ij}\hskip 0.85358pt,\quad(i,j\!=\!1,2,3),

(149)

and all other components vanish. Using the formula

\left(\Sigma^{0i}\right)_{\alpha}^{\,\,\,\beta}=\!-\!\left(\Sigma^{i0}\right)_{\alpha}^{\,\,\,\beta}=\frac{1}{4}\!\left(\sigma^{0}\bar{\sigma}^{i}\!-\!\sigma^{i}\bar{\sigma}^{0}\right)_{\alpha}^{\,\,\,\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\!\left(\sigma^{0}\bar{\sigma}^{i}\right)_{\alpha}^{\ \beta},

(150)

we simplify the covariant derivative (acting on the Weyl spinor) as follows:

\mathrm{D}_{0}\psi_{\alpha}\!=\partial_{0}\psi_{\alpha}\hskip 0.85358pt,\quad\mathrm{D}_{i}\psi_{\alpha}=\partial_{i}\psi_{\alpha}\!-\!\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}\big(\sigma^{0}\bar{\sigma}^{i}\big)_{\alpha}^{\ \beta}\psi_{\beta}\,,

(151)

where
\sigma^{a}
and
\bar{\sigma}^{a}
are the sigma matrices in the flat spacetime.

Consider the free Lagrangian of a Weyl spinor in the curved spacetime,

\Delta\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{-g\,}\;\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\psi_{{\dot{\alpha}}}^{\dagger}\bar{\varsigma}^{\hskip 0.85358pt\mu{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\psi_{\alpha}\,,

(152)

where the Pauli sigma matrices
\varsigma_{\mu}

and
\bar{\varsigma}^{\mu}
are defined
from the flat-space sigma matrices
\sigma^{a}

through the vierbein
e_{a}^{\ \mu}
,

\displaystyle\varsigma^{\mu}=e_{b}^{\ \mu}\sigma^{b},\,\hskip 17.07164pt\bar{\varsigma}^{\mu}=e_{b}^{\ \mu}\bar{\sigma}^{b}\,.

(153)

For the FLRW universe, the Pauli sigma matrices
\varsigma^{\mu}

and
\bar{\varsigma}^{\mu}
are expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\varsigma^{\mu}=a(\tau)^{-1}\sigma^{\mu},\,\hskip 17.07164pt\bar{\varsigma}^{\mu}=a(\tau)^{-1}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\,.

(154)

Then, the kinematic term of the free Lagrangian for a Weyl spinor in FLRW spacetime
takes the following form:

\Delta\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{-g\,}\frac{\,\mathrm{i}\,}{\,a\,}\psi_{{\dot{\alpha}}}^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}\text{D}_{\mu}\psi_{\alpha}\,.

(155)

Using
\sqrt{-g\,}\!=\!a(\tau)^{4}
and the covariant derivative formula
(
151
), we derive the above Lagrangian as follows:

\Delta\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pta^{3}\psi^{\dagger}\!\hskip-0.85358pt\left(\!\bar{\sigma}^{0}\partial_{0}\!+\!\bar{\sigma}^{i}\partial_{i}\!+\!\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}\bar{\sigma}^{0}\!\hskip-0.85358pt\right)\hskip-0.85358pt\!\psi\,,

(156)

where we use an identity,

\sum_{i}\hskip-0.85358pt(\bar{\sigma}^{i}\sigma^{0}\bar{\sigma}^{i})^{{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}=-3\bar{\sigma}^{0{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}\,.

(157)

With the field redefinition

\tilde{\psi}=a^{3/2}\psi\,,

(158)

we can simplify the Lagrangian to the flat-space form:

\Delta\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\psi}^{\dagger}(\bar{\sigma}^{0}\partial_{0}+\bar{\sigma}^{i}\partial_{i})\tilde{\psi}=\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\psi}^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{\psi}\,.

(159)

Thus, using the field redefinition for the right-handed neutrino field

\tilde{N}\!\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\!a^{3/2}N
, we can simplify the free Lagrangian
(
20
) of the right-handed neutrinos
with a chemical potential as follows:

\Delta\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt{\tilde{N}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}}}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{N}_{\alpha}\!-\!a\lambda{\tilde{N}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}}}\bar{\sigma}^{0{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}\tilde{N}_{\alpha}\!-\!\frac{1}{2}aM_{\!R}(\tilde{N}^{\alpha}\tilde{N}_{\alpha}\!+\!\tilde{N}^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}{\tilde{N}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}}})\,.

(160)

Then, we derive the equation of motion for
\tilde{N}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}}
,

\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\bar{\sigma}^{\mu{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{N}_{\alpha}=a\lambda\bar{\sigma}^{0{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}\tilde{N}_{\alpha}+aM_{\!R}\tilde{N}^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}\,.

(161)

With this, we define the quantized right-handed-neutrino fields:

\displaystyle\tilde{N}_{\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\frac{\text{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{s=\pm}\!\left[\xi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})b_{s}(\mathbf{k})e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\chi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})b_{s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\right]\!,

(162)

\displaystyle\tilde{N}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}}(\tau,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\frac{\text{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{s=\pm}\!\left[\xi^{\dagger}_{s,{\dot{\alpha}}}(\tau,\mathbf{k})b_{s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\chi^{\dagger}_{s,{\dot{\alpha}}}(\tau,\mathbf{k})b_{s}(\mathbf{k})e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\right]\!,

where
b_{s}
and
b_{s}^{\dagger}
are annihilation and creation operators satisfying

\{b_{s}(\mathbf{k}),b_{s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k^{\prime}})\}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}\delta^{3}(\mathbf{k}\!-\!\mathbf{k^{\prime}})\,.

(163)

In the above expansions (
162
),

\xi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})
and
\chi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})
are the mode functions. Substituting Eq.(
162
) into the equation of motion
(
160
), we derive the equations of motion for the mode functions:

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt(\bar{\sigma}^{0\,{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}\xi^{\prime}_{s,\beta}\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\bar{\sigma}^{i\,\dot{\alpha}\beta}k_{i}\xi_{s,\beta})

\displaystyle=a\lambda\bar{\sigma}^{0\,{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}\xi_{s,\beta}\!+\!aM_{\!R}\chi^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}}_{s}\,,

(164a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt(\bar{\sigma}^{0\,{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}\chi^{\prime}_{s,\beta}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\bar{\sigma}^{i\,\dot{\alpha}\beta}k_{i}\chi_{s,\beta})

\displaystyle=a\lambda\bar{\sigma}^{0\,{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}\chi_{s,\beta}\!+\!aM_{\!R}\xi^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}}_{s}\,,

(164b)

where we have used the notation
f^{\prime}(\tau)\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\text{d}f(\tau)/\text{d}\tau\hskip 0.85358pt

with
f(\tau)\!=\!\xi_{s,\beta}(\tau)
or

f(\tau)\!=\!\chi_{s,\beta}(\tau)
. We can reexpress the above second equation and have the following coupled equations of motion
for the two mode functions
\xi_{s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
and

\chi_{s}^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}}(\mathbf{k})
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt(\bar{\sigma}^{0\,{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}\xi^{\prime}_{s,\beta}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\bar{\sigma}^{i\,\dot{\alpha}\beta}k_{i}\xi_{s,\beta})

\displaystyle=a\lambda\bar{\sigma}^{0\,{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}\xi_{s,\beta}\!+\!aM_{\!R}\chi^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}}_{s}\,,

(165a)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt(-\sigma^{0}_{\beta\dot{\alpha}}\chi^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}\,\prime}_{s}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk_{i}\sigma^{i}_{\beta\dot{\alpha}}\chi^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}}_{s})

\displaystyle=-a\lambda\sigma^{0}_{\beta\dot{\alpha}}\chi^{\dagger\dot{\alpha}}_{s}\!+\!aM_{\!R}\xi_{s,\beta}\,.

(165b)

We further factorize the helicity spinors and define the spinor coefficients

u_{s}
and
v_{s}
as follows:

\xi_{s,\alpha}(\tau,\mathbf{k})=u_{s}(\tau,k)h_{s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,\quad~\chi^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}_{s}(\tau,\mathbf{k})=v_{s}(\tau,k)\hskip 0.85358pts\,h^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}_{-s}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,

(166)

where

s\,h^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}_{-s}(\mathbf{k})=\pm h^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}_{\mp}(\mathbf{k})

for
s\!=\!\pm\,
. The unit helicity eigenspinors
h_{s}
for momentum
\mathbf{k}
in the direction

\hat{\mathbf{\,k}}=(\sin\theta\cos\varphi,\sin\theta\sin\varphi,\cos\theta)

(167)

are chosen to take the following forms:

\displaystyle\hskip-9.95845pth_{+,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=h_{+,\alpha}(\theta,\varphi)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\cos\!\frac{\theta}{2}\\[5.69054pt]
e^{+\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\sin\!\frac{\theta}{2}\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\!\alpha}\!,

(168a)

\displaystyle h_{-,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=h_{-,\alpha}(\theta,\varphi)=\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\sin\!\frac{\theta}{2}\\[5.69054pt]
e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\cos\!\frac{\theta}{2}\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\!\alpha}\!.

(168b)

We also have the relation

h_{s,\alpha}\!=\!s\hskip 0.85358pth^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}_{-s}
,
which leads to the following:

h^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}_{-}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\cos\!\frac{\theta}{2}\\[5.69054pt]
e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\sin\!\frac{\theta}{2}\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\!{\dot{\alpha}}}\!,\quad-h^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}_{+}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}\!-e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\sin\!\frac{\theta}{2}\\[5.69054pt]
\!e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\varphi}{2}}\!\cos\!\frac{\theta}{2}\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\!{\dot{\alpha}}}\!.

(169)

The helicity eigenspinors
h_{s,\alpha}
satisfy the following relations:

\displaystyle\big(\!-\!\vec{\bar{\sigma}}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{k}}\big)^{\!{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}h_{s,\beta}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=h_{-s}^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,\hskip 19.91692pt\bar{\sigma}^{0\,{\dot{\alpha}}\beta}h_{s,\beta}(\mathbf{k})=sh_{-s}^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,

(170a)

\displaystyle\big(\vec{\sigma}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{k}}\big)_{\!\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}h^{\dagger{\dot{\beta}}}_{s}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=h_{-s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,\hskip 21.05501pt\sigma^{0}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}h^{\dagger{\dot{\beta}}}_{s}(\mathbf{k})=-sh_{-s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,

(170b)

which is consistent with Eq.(
26
). In the above equations, we express the spinor indices explicitly,
with
\alpha
denoting left-handed
(\frac{\,1\,}{\,2\,},0)
indices
and
{\dot{\alpha}}
denoting right-handed
(0,\frac{\,1\,}{\,2\,})
indices.

Substituting Eq.(
166
) into the equations of motion (
165
),
we derive the following coupled equations for the spinor coefficients

u_{s}
and
v_{s}\hskip 0.85358pt
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptu^{\prime}_{\pm}\hskip-0.85358pt\pm\hskip-0.85358ptku_{\pm}

\displaystyle=a\lambda u_{\pm}\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358ptaM_{\!R}v_{\pm}\,,

(171a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptv^{\prime}_{\pm}\hskip-0.85358pt\mp\hskip-0.85358ptkv_{\pm}

\displaystyle=-a\lambda v_{\pm}\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358ptaM_{\!R}u_{\pm}\,.

(171b)

Then, we recast these two equations into the diagonal forms:

\displaystyle u^{\prime\prime}_{\pm}\!-\!aHu^{\prime}_{\pm}\!+\!\left[(\pm k\!-\!a\lambda)^{2}\!+\!a^{2}M_{\!R}^{2}\hskip-0.85358pt\pm\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaHk\right]\!u_{\pm}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(172a)

\displaystyle v^{\prime\prime}_{\pm}\!-\!aHv^{\prime}_{\pm}\!+\!\left[(\mp k\!+\!a\lambda)^{2}\!+\!a^{2}M_{\!R}^{2}\hskip-0.85358pt\mp\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptaHk\right]\!v_{\pm}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(172b)

The above second-order differential equations just give Eqs.(
30a
)
in Section
2.2
, which can be solved to give the solutions
in Eq.(
31
).

Appendix B
Schwinger-Keldysh Propagators of Weyl Spinors

In this Appendix, we briefly summarize the propagators of two-component Weyl spinors
in the Schwinger-Keldysh (SK) path-integral formalism. We present the generating functional and the fermion propagators for Weyl spinors
in the presence of a nonzero chemical potential.

Since the path-integral variables for fermion fields are anticommuting Grassmann quantities,
some care is required for constructing the doubled path integral in the in-in formalism. In particular, the reverse-time-ordered contour should be placed to the left-hand-side
of the time-ordered contour. Let
\psi_{+}
and
\psi_{-}
denote the path-integral variables on the time-ordered and reverse-time-ordered contours respectively,
and let
I_{+}
and
I_{-}
be the corresponding external sources. Then, the generating functional of the in-in correlators can be written as follows:

\displaystyle Z[I_{-},I_{-}^{\dagger};I_{+},I_{+}^{\dagger}]=

\displaystyle\left.\int\!\!\mathcal{D}\psi_{-}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}\psi_{-}\exp\!\left[-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptS[\psi_{-},\psi_{-}^{\dagger}]\hskip-0.85358pt-\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\!\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\Big(I_{-}^{\alpha}\psi_{-\alpha}\!+\!I_{-{\dot{\alpha}}}^{\dagger}\psi_{-}^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}\Big)\!\right]\right.

(173)

\displaystyle\times\!\int\!\!\mathcal{D}\psi_{+}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}\psi_{+}\exp\!\left[\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptS[\psi_{+},\psi_{+}^{\dagger}]\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\!\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\Big(I_{+}^{\alpha}\psi_{+\alpha}\!+\!I_{+{\dot{\alpha}}}^{\dagger}\psi_{+}^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}\Big)\!\right]\!.

In the above path integral, we treat the left-handed spinors
\psi_{\pm\alpha}
and the
right-handed spinors
\psi_{\pm}^{\dagger a}
as independent variables. It is understood that the boundary conditions
\psi_{+}\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\psi_{-}

and
\psi_{+}^{\dagger}\!=\psi_{-}^{\dagger}
at future infinity
\tau\!=\!0
are imposed.

From the generating functional (
173
),
we derive three types of two-point correlators (propagators)
of the Weyl spinors
\psi
and
\psi^{\dagger}

by taking two functional derivatives with respect to
the corresponding external sources:

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{ab\alpha\beta}\!\left(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x};\tau_{2},\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\!\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\frac{\delta}{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pta\delta I_{a}^{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x})\,}\frac{\delta}{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358pt\delta I_{b}^{\beta}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{y})\,}Z\big[I_{-},I_{-}^{\dagger};I_{+},I_{+}^{\dagger}\big]\hskip-0.85358pt\bigg|_{I=0},

(174a)

\displaystyle\check{D}_{ab{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x};\tau_{2},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\!\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\,\mathbf{x}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\frac{\delta}{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pta\delta I_{a}^{\dagger{\dot{\alpha}}}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x})\,}\frac{\delta}{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358pt\delta I_{b}^{\dagger{\dot{\beta}}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{y})\,}Z\big[I_{-},I_{-}^{\dagger};I_{+},I_{+}^{\dagger}\big]\bigg|_{I=0},

(174b)

\displaystyle D_{ab\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\left(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x};\tau_{2},\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\!\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\frac{\delta}{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pta\delta I_{a}^{\alpha}\left(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x}\right)\,}\frac{\delta}{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358pt\delta I_{b}^{\dagger{\dot{\beta}}}\left(\tau_{2},\mathbf{y}\right)\,}Z\big[I_{-},I_{-}^{\dagger};I_{+},I_{+}^{\dagger}\big]\bigg|_{I=0}.

(174c)

These objects correspond to the two-point vacuum expectation values in the in-in formalism. For instance, the propagators of the
D_{ab\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}
type are given by

\displaystyle D_{\ominus\oplus\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\left(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\langle 0|\psi_{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x})\psi^{\dagger}_{{\dot{\beta}}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{0})|0\rangle\,,

(175a)

\displaystyle D_{\oplus\ominus\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\left(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\!-\!\!\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\langle 0|\psi^{\dagger}_{{\dot{\beta}}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{0})\psi_{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x})|0\rangle\,,

(175b)

\displaystyle D_{\oplus\oplus\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\left(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\langle 0|\mathbf{T}\Big\{\psi_{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x})\psi^{\dagger}_{{\dot{\beta}}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{0})\!\Big\}|0\rangle\,,

(175c)

\displaystyle D_{\ominus\ominus\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\left(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\langle 0|\bar{\mathbf{T}}\Big\{\!\psi_{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x})\psi^{\dagger}_{{\dot{\beta}}}(\tau_{2},\mathbf{0})\!\Big\}|0\rangle\,,

(175d)

where
\hskip 0.85358pt\mathbf{T}\hskip 0.85358pt
denotes the time-ordered product and
\bar{\mathbf{T}}

the reverse-time-ordered product. The extra minus sign in
D_{\oplus\ominus\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}

arises from exchanging the fermionic fields when the reverse-time-ordered field is placed
to the left of the time-ordered field.

Substituting the mode expansion of
\psi_{\alpha}
and
\psi^{\dagger}_{{\dot{\beta}}}
of Eq.(
23
) into the above definitions,
we find that only the term containing
b_{s}(\mathbf{k})b_{s}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})
survives because

\{b_{s}(\mathbf{k}),b_{s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k^{\prime}})\}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}\delta^{3}(\mathbf{k}\!-\!\mathbf{k^{\prime}})\,,

(176)

with which the fermion propagators can be derived as in
Section
2.3
.

Appendix C
Derivations of the Relevant Formulas

In this Appendix, we present several derivations for the evaluation of the right-handed-neutrino loop contribution to the three-point inflaton correlation function. Our purpose is to make
the calculation strategy more transparent and to separate the basic derivations from the
analysis of special kinematic limits.

We proceed in the following order. First, we summarize several general relations that are
repeatedly used in the loop calculation, including the conjugation properties of the external
lines and loop diagrams. Then, we present sample derivations of the seed integrals introduced in Section
2.4
. These seed integrals form the basic
building blocks of the factorized loop calculation. We further explain how
to use these seed integrals to form the three-point inflaton correlators and clarify
the origin of the dominant contribution in the squeezed limit. After that, we discuss the folded-limit behavior and the cutting rule,
which provide a useful structural interpretation of the nonlocal part of the signal. Finally, we derive the bubble loop integral
by using the Feynman diagram approach.

C.1
Conjugation Relations between Three-Point Correlators

In this subsection,
we analyze the relations between the three-point loop correlators. Specifically, we can classify the eight diagrams of Fig.
2

into four pairs, where the two diagrams within each pair are
related by complex conjugation.
A particularly useful relation is the conjugation property of the external function
(
83
),

\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(\mathbf{k},\tau)^{*}=\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\text{-}a}(-\mathbf{k},\tau)\,,

(177)

which follows directly from the definition of the SK bulk-to-boundary propagator (
84
). In consequence, the pairs of correlators related by reversing the fermion-flow in the loop
are complex conjugates of each other. This relation can also be checked numerically.

As an illustration, consider diagram-(7) of Fig.
2
, whose contribution takes
the following form:

\displaystyle\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(7)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b,c=\pm}\!\!a\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358ptc\left(\!\frac{\,-\mathrm{i}\,}{\,\Lambda\,}\!\right)^{\!\!3}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}{\text{d}}\tau_{1}{\text{d}}\tau_{2}{\text{d}}\tau_{3}

(178)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(\mathbf{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\mathcal{F}_{\nu b}(\mathbf{k}_{2},\tau_{2})\mathcal{F}_{\lambda c}(\mathbf{k}_{3},\tau_{3})\!\!\int\!\!\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}\,}{(2\pi)^{3}}\mathcal{Y}_{abc}^{\mu\nu\lambda}(7)\,,

where the function
\mathcal{Y}_{abc}^{\mu\nu\lambda}(7)
is given by

\mathcal{Y}_{abc}^{\mu\nu\lambda}(7)=\bar{\sigma}^{\mu{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}\hat{D}_{ab\alpha}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\beta}(\mathbf{p}_{12},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\sigma^{\nu}_{\,\,\,\beta{\dot{\beta}}}\check{D}_{bc\,\,\,{\dot{\gamma}}}^{\,\,\,\,\,{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{p}_{23},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})\bar{\sigma}^{\lambda{\dot{\gamma}}\gamma}D_{ca\gamma{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{p}_{31},\tau_{3},\tau_{1})\,,

(179)

with
\mathbf{p}_{12}\!=\!\mathbf{q}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{1}
,

\mathbf{p}_{23}\!=\!\mathbf{q}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{s}
,
and
\mathbf{p}_{31}\hskip-0.85358pt\!=\hskip-0.85358pt\mathbf{q}\hskip 0.85358pt
.

Taking the complex conjugate of Eq.(
178
), we derive the following:

\displaystyle\left[\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(7)}\right]^{*}=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b,c=\pm}\!a\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358ptc\left(\!\frac{\,-\mathrm{i}\,}{\,\Lambda\,}\!\right)^{\!\!3}\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\mathrm{d}\tau_{3}

(180)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(\mathbf{k}_{1},\tau_{1})^{*}\mathcal{F}_{\nu b}(\mathbf{k}_{2},\tau_{2})^{*}\mathcal{F}_{\lambda c}(\mathbf{k}_{3},\tau_{3})^{*}\int\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,}\mathcal{Y}_{abc}^{\mu\nu\lambda}(7)^{*}\,.

Using

\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(\mathbf{k},\tau)^{*}=\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\text{-}a}(-\mathbf{k},\tau)
,
we deduce the following:

\displaystyle\left[\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(7)}\right]^{*}=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b,c=\pm}\!a\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358ptc\left(\!\frac{\,-\mathrm{i}\,}{\,\Lambda\,}\!\right)^{\!\!3}\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\mathrm{d}\tau_{3}

(181)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(-\mathbf{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\mathcal{F}_{\nu b}(-\mathbf{k}_{2},\tau_{2})\mathcal{F}_{\lambda c}(-\mathbf{k}_{3},\tau_{3})\!\int\!\!\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}\,}\mathcal{Y}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b,\text{-}c}^{\mu\nu\lambda}(7)^{*}\,.

To evaluate
\mathcal{Y}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b,\text{-}c}^{\mu\nu\lambda}(7)^{\!*}
,
it is convenient to suppress spinor indices and use the following trace notation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b,\text{-}c}^{\mu\nu\lambda}(7)^{*}

\displaystyle=\left[\text{Tr}\!\left(\hskip-0.85358pt\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\hskip-0.85358pt\hat{D}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b}(\mathbf{p}_{12},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\sigma^{\nu}\check{D}_{\text{-}b,\text{-}c}(\mathbf{p}_{23},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})\bar{\sigma}^{\lambda}D_{\text{-}c,\text{-}a}(\mathbf{p}_{31},\tau_{3},\tau_{1})\hskip-0.85358pt\right)\hskip-1.42262pt\right]^{*}

(182)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left\{\!\sigma^{\nu}\!\left[\hat{D}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b}(\mathbf{p}_{12},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]^{\dagger}\!\!\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\!\left[D_{\text{-}c,\text{-}a}(\mathbf{p}_{31},\tau_{3},\tau_{1})\right]^{\dagger}\!\bar{\sigma}^{\lambda}\!\left[\check{D}_{\text{-}b,\text{-}c}(\mathbf{p}_{23},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})\right]^{\!\dagger}\!\right\}\!.

The Hermitian conjugates of the propagators satisfy the relations:

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\left[\hat{D}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b\,\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\hskip-0.85358pt\!=\!\check{D}_{ba{\dot{\beta}}{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\left[\hat{D}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b\,\alpha}^{\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996pt\beta}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\hskip-0.85358pt\!=\!-\check{D}_{ba\hskip 8.04996pt\!\!\!\!{\dot{\alpha}}}^{\hskip 8.04996pt\!\!{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\left[\check{D}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b\,{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\hskip-0.85358pt\!=\!\hat{D}_{ba\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\left[\check{D}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b\hskip 8.04996pt\!\!\!\!\!{\dot{\beta}}}^{\hskip 8.04996pt\,\,\,{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\hskip-0.85358pt\!=\!-\hat{D}_{ba\,\beta}^{\hskip 8.04996pt\,\,\alpha}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\,,

(183)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\left[D_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b\,\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\hskip-0.85358pt\!=\!D_{ba\beta{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{p},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\,.

Thus, using the above relations, we further derive Eq.(
182
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\text{-}a,\text{-}b,\text{-}c}^{\mu\nu\lambda}(7)^{*}

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\left\{\sigma^{\nu}\check{D}_{ba}(\mathbf{p}_{12},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}D_{ac}(\mathbf{p}_{31},\tau_{1},\tau_{3})\bar{\sigma}^{\lambda}\hat{D}_{cb}(\mathbf{p}_{23},\tau_{3},\tau_{2})\right\}

(184)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\nu}_{\,\,\,\alpha{\dot{\alpha}}}\check{D}_{ba\,\,\,{\dot{\beta}}}^{\,\,\,\,\,{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{p}_{12},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\bar{\sigma}^{\mu{\dot{\beta}}\beta}D_{ac\beta{\dot{\gamma}}}(\mathbf{p}_{31},\tau_{1},\tau_{3})\bar{\sigma}^{\lambda{\dot{\gamma}}\gamma}\hat{D}_{cb\gamma}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\alpha}(\mathbf{p}_{23},\tau_{3},\tau_{2})\,.

Then, we reverse the direction of each external momentum as
\mathbf{k}_{j}\rightarrow-\mathbf{k}_{j}
,
and further reverse the direction of the loop momentum
\mathbf{q}\rightarrow-\mathbf{q}
. With these and using Eq.(
184
),
we further express the three-point correlator (
181
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left[\langle\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(7)}\right]^{\hskip-0.85358pt*}

(185)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c=\pm}\!a\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358ptc\left(\!\frac{\,-\mathrm{i}\,}{\,\Lambda\,}\!\right)^{\!\!3}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\mathrm{d}\tau_{3}\,\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(\mathbf{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\mathcal{F}_{\nu b}(\mathbf{k}_{2},\tau_{2})\mathcal{F}_{\lambda c}(\mathbf{k}_{3},\tau_{3})

\displaystyle\times\!\!\int\!\!\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}\,}{\,(2\pi)^{3}\,}\sigma^{\nu}_{\,\,\,\alpha{\dot{\alpha}}}\check{D}_{ba\,\,\,{\dot{\beta}}}^{\,\,\,\,\,{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{p}_{21},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\bar{\sigma}^{\mu{\dot{\beta}}\beta}D_{ac\beta{\dot{\gamma}}}(\mathbf{p}_{13},\tau_{1},\tau_{3})\bar{\sigma}^{\lambda{\dot{\gamma}}\gamma}\hat{D}_{cb\gamma}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\alpha}(\mathbf{p}_{32},\tau_{3},\tau_{2})\,,

which just describes the contribution from the diagram-(5) of Fig.
2
. Hence, we deduce the following relation:

\left[\hskip-0.85358pt\langle\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(7)}\hskip-0.85358pt\right]^{\hskip-0.85358pt*}=\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(5)}\,.

(186)

Note that the cosmological collider signal takes the schematic form in the squeezed limit,

\lim_{k_{s}\!\rightarrow 0}\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime}_{\text{(NLoc)}}\thicksim\frac{A}{\,k_{1}^{3}k_{3}^{3}\,}\!\left(\!\frac{\,k_{1}\,}{\,k_{3}\,}\!\right)^{\!\!n+\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}}\!f(\hat{\,\mathbf{k}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{\,\mathbf{k}}_{3})+\!\!\text{ H.c. }\!,

(187)

where
n
is certain real number and the function
f(\hat{\,\mathbf{k}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\hat{\,\mathbf{k}}_{3})

depends only on the angle between the two external momenta. Thus, the signal should
be invariant under the transformation
\mathbf{k}_{j}\!\rightarrow\!-\mathbf{k}_{j}
,
with which we derive the following:

\displaystyle\left[\lim_{k_{s}\!\rightarrow 0}\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(7)}_{\text{(NLoc)}}\right]^{*}

\displaystyle=\left[\lim_{k_{s}\!\rightarrow 0}\langle\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(-\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(7)}_{\text{(NLoc)}}\right]^{*}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k_{s}\!\rightarrow 0}\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime(5)}_{\text{(NLoc)}}\,,

(188)

where in the last step we have used Eq.(
186
). Moreover, we can prove that for other pairs of diagrams in Fig.
2
,
including each pair of diagrams-(1)(2), (3)(4), and (6)(8), also obey the same type of relations
as derived in (
130
).

C.2
Derivation of Seed Integrals for Fermion Propagators

In Section
2.4
, we introduced the seed integrals
associated with the three types of fermion propagators and listed the entries of
the corresponding seed-integral matrices, such as
A_{i}^{p_{1},p_{2}}
​,
S_{i}^{p_{1},p_{2}}
​,

U_{i}^{p_{1},p_{2}}
​, and
W_{i}^{p_{1},p_{2}}
(with
i\!=\!1,2
). In this subsection, we derive these entries explicitly. As representative examples, we present the derivation of the time-ordered integral

S_{1}^{p_{1},p_{2}}
and the non-time-ordered integral
W_{1}^{p_{1},p_{2}}
. The remaining entries can be obtained analogously.

\blacklozenge
Time-Ordered Seed Integral:

We first consider the following time-ordered integral
S_{1}^{p_{1},p_{2}}
,

\displaystyle S_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}\,u_{+}(z_{1})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})\theta(z_{2}\!-\!z_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{z_{1}}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{+}(z_{1})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt(z+z_{1})}z_{1}^{p_{1}}(z\!+\!z_{1})^{p_{2}}\,u_{+}(z_{1})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z\!+\!z_{1})\,.

(189)

where
(p_{1},\hskip 0.85358ptp_{2})
are the relevant powers associated with
(z_{1},\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})
. In the last step of the above derivation, we have changed the integral variable
z_{2}

by
z\!=\!z_{2}\!-\!z_{1}
, whereas the spinor coefficients

u_{+}
and
v_{+}
are given by

\displaystyle u_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\,e^{+\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}\,}{\sqrt{2z\,}\,}\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358ptW_{\!-\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda},\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2z)\,,

(190a)

\displaystyle v_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\,e^{+\pi\tilde{\lambda}/2}\,}{\sqrt{2z\,}\,}W_{\!\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\lambda},\mathrm{i}\tilde{\nu}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2z)\,.

(190b)

The power factors
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are complex in general and
all such complex power are defined on the principal branch,

w^{p}\equiv e^{p\log w},\qquad\arg(w)\in(-\pi,\pi].

(191)

In particular, for
x\!>\!0\hskip 0.85358pt
, we have

(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptx)^{p}\!=\!e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi p/2}x^{p}
.

For Eq.(
189
), we first make Wick rotation
z\!\!\rightarrow\!\!-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz

for the integral contour of
z\hskip 0.85358pt
. Since
z_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz
stays in the fourth quadrant for
z_{1}\!>\!0
and
z\!>\!0
,
the contour deformation does not cross the branch cut:

\displaystyle S_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}u_{+}(z_{1})\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z\,(-\mathrm{i})\,e^{-\mathrm{i}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz+z_{1})}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz\!+\!z_{1})^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz\!+\!z_{1})\,.

(192)

The subsequent Wick rotation of the
z_{1}
contour is more delicate, because
u_{+}(z_{1})
contains the Whittaker function
according to the expression (
190a
),
which is a function of
z_{1}
and has a branch cut along the negative imaginary axis. Hence, the contour deformation should be understood as follows:

z_{1}\rightarrow e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt(\pi/2-\epsilon)}z_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt,\qquad(\epsilon\!>\!0),

(193)

and the limit
\epsilon\!\rightarrow\!0^{+}
is taken after the deformation.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 11
:
Domain-coloring plots illustrating the origin of the branch cut in the
z_{1}
plane:
(a).
W_{-\frac{1}{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda},\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2z_{1})
,
(b).
{}_{1}F_{1}(1\!\hskip 0.85358pt+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\hskip-0.85358pt-\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu};-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2z_{1})
, and
(c).
(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{1/2\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}
. The horizontal and vertical axes denote
\mathrm{Re}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}
and
\mathrm{Im}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}
, respectively. The color indicates the complex phase, whereas the brightness indicates the magnitude. Plot (b) is regular in the
z_{1}
plane, whereas the non-analyticity in plot (a)
is associated with the fractional-power behavior shown in plot (c),
whose principal-branch cut lies along the negative imaginary axis of the
z_{1}
plane.

To identify the origin of the branch cut, we rewrite the Whittaker function
in terms of the confluent hypergeometric function
{}_{1}F_{1}
by using Eq.(
33
):

\displaystyle\hskip-9.95845ptW_{\!-\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda},\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2z_{1})=\frac{\,2^{\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\,}{\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}{}_{1}F_{1}(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu};-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2z_{1})\!+\!(\tilde{\nu}\!\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\hskip 0.85358pt.

(194)

Since
{}_{1}F_{1}(a;b;x)
is analytic in
x
and does not generate branch cuts,
the non-analyticity in the
z_{1}
plane originates from the fractional-power factor

(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{1/2\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}
in Eq.(
194
),
as illustrated in Fig.
11
. With the principal-branch convention adopted above, this factor has a branch cut
along the negative imaginary axis of the
z_{1}
plane. Hence, the Wick rotation of the
z_{1}
contour should be understood as approaching this axis
from the fourth quadrant, namely, through the regulated deformation

z_{1}\!\rightarrow\!e^{-\mathrm{i}(\pi/2-\epsilon)}z_{1}
, with
\epsilon\!\rightarrow\!0^{+}
. After the principal branch has been fixed by this prescription,
the Wick rotation of each fractional-power factor appearing in Eq.(
194
)
can be evaluated as

(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{\alpha}\!\rightarrow\!\left(e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi}z_{1}\right)^{\alpha}\!=\!e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi\alpha}z_{1}^{\alpha}\hskip 0.85358pt
. With this prescription and making the Wick rotation
z_{1}\!\!\rightarrow\!\!-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}

for the integral contour of
z_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptS_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}(-\mathrm{i})\,e^{-z_{1}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{p_{1}}u_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z\,(-\mathrm{i})\,e^{-(z+z_{1})}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})

\displaystyle=-\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-z_{1}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{p_{1}}u_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z\,e^{-(z+z_{1})}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\hskip 0.85358pt,

(195)

where the overall minus sign comes from the two Jacobian factors,
(-\mathrm{i})^{2}\!=\!-1
.

Finally, defining
z^{\prime}\!=\!z\!+\!z_{1}
and
\text{d}z^{\prime}\!=\!\text{d}z
, we further derive

\displaystyle S_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-z_{1}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{p_{1}}u_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\!\int_{z_{1}}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z^{\prime}\,e^{-z^{\prime}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz^{\prime})^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-z_{1}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{p_{1}}u_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\!\int_{z_{1}}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-z_{2}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt.

(196)

Equivalently, using the principal-branch relation

(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptx)^{p}\!=\!e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi p/2}x^{p}
(for
x\!>\!0
), we obtain the following:

S_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=-e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi(p_{1}+p_{2})}\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-z_{1}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}u_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\!\int_{z_{1}}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-z_{2}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt.

(197)

Next, we proceed to explicitly evaluate the double integral over
z_{1}
and
z_{2}

in Eq.(
197
). Since the
z_{2}
integration is nested within the
z_{1}
integration,
we begin with the
z_{2}
integral. The integral over
z_{2}
can be evaluated analytically,
yielding an indefinite integral in terms of a Meijer
G
function. Taking the asymptotic expansion at infinity and then setting the lower limit to

z_{2}\!=\!z_{1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}(z_{1})

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{z_{1}}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-z_{2}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})

\displaystyle=2^{-\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}-p_{2}}e^{\frac{\,\pi\tilde{\lambda}\,}{2}}e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\,\pi\,}{4}}z_{2}^{-\frac{\,1\,}{\,2\,}}G^{2,1}_{2,3}\!\!\left.\left[\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,},\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\\
\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\!+\!p_{2}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\frac{\,1\,}{\,2\,}\end{array}\!\!;2z_{2}\right]\right|_{z_{1}}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\!2^{-1-p_{2}}e^{\frac{\,\pi\tilde{\lambda}\,}{2}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\,\pi\,}{4}}\frac{\,\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}{{\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})}}\!\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\bigg(2^{-\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}-p_{2}}e^{\frac{\,\pi\tilde{\lambda}\,}{2}}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\,\pi\,}{4}}z_{1}^{-\frac{\,1\,}{\,2\,}}G^{2,1}_{2,3}\!\hskip-0.85358pt\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,},\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\\
\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\!+\!p_{2}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\frac{\,1\,}{\,2\,}\end{array}\!\!;2z_{1}\right]\!\hskip-0.85358pt\bigg).

The Meijer
G
function is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38109ptG^{m,n}_{p,q}\!\!\left[\!\begin{array}[]{c}a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots,b_{q}\end{array};z\right]\equiv\frac{1}{\,2\pi\mathrm{i}\,}\!\!\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle C$}}$}}\nolimits\!\!\text{d}s\,z^{s}\frac{~\bigg[\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{n}\!\Gamma(1\!-\!a_{k}\!+\!s)\bigg]\hskip-0.85358pt\bigg/\hskip-0.85358pt\bigg[\!\displaystyle\prod_{k=m+1}^{q}\!\!\!\Gamma(1\!-\!b_{k}\!+\!s)\bigg]~}{\bigg[\!\displaystyle\prod_{k=n+1}^{p}\!\!\!\Gamma(a_{k}\!-\!s)\!\bigg]\hskip-0.85358pt\bigg/\hskip-0.85358pt\bigg[\!\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\!\Gamma(b_{k}\!-\!s)\hskip-0.85358pt\bigg]}.

In the second step, we evaluate the integral over
z_{1}
from
0
to
\infty
. By virtue of Eq.(
33
) and after adopting the branch-cut prescription specified above,

u_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})
can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-17.07164ptu_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})=\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{z_{1}}e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi}{4}}e^{\frac{\,\pi\tilde{\lambda}\,}{2}}\!\Bigg[\!\frac{\,2^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}e^{-\pi\tilde{\nu}}z_{1}^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\,}{\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}{}_{1}F_{1}(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu};-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2z_{1})\!+\!(\tilde{\nu}\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\!\Bigg]\!.

(204)

Then, we can evaluate the remaining
z_{1}
integral directly, which yields the following:

\displaystyle S_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle-\!e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi(p_{1}+p_{2})}\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-z_{1}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}u_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\mathcal{I}_{2}(z_{1})

(205)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi\,2^{-2-p_{1}-p_{2}}e^{-\frac{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi\,}{2}(p_{1}+p_{2})}e^{\pi\tilde{\lambda}}\tilde{M}_{\!R}\,\text{csch}(2\pi\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2})\,}{\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})}

\displaystyle\times\Bigg[e^{-\pi\tilde{\nu}}\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})

\displaystyle\times{}_{4}\tilde{F}_{3}\!\left(\begin{matrix}2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2},\;1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\;1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\;2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\\
2\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\;2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu},\;1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}\end{matrix};-1\!\hskip-0.85358pt\right)\!-\!(\tilde{\nu}\rightarrow\!-\tilde{\nu})\hskip-0.85358pt\Bigg].

\blacklozenge
Non-Time-Ordered Seed Integral:

Next, we consider the following non-time-ordered integral
W_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
,

\displaystyle W_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}u_{+}(z_{1})v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})

(206)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}u_{+}(z_{1})\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}v_{+}^{\dagger}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})^{p_{1}}u_{+}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-z_{2}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi\,}{2}(p_{1}-p_{2})}\!\!\hskip-0.85358pt\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-z_{1}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}u_{+}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-z_{2}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})\,.

Unlike the time-ordered integrals, the variables
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are not coupled in the non-time-ordered case,
so the double integral factorizes into a product of two single integrals:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{1}\,e^{-z_{1}}z_{1}^{p_{1}}u_{+}(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{1})=(1\!-\!\mathrm{i})2^{-\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}-p_{1}}e^{\frac{\,\pi\tilde{\lambda}\,}{2}}\tilde{M}_{\!R}\frac{\,\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}{\,\Gamma(2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})\,}\,,

(207a)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\text{d}z_{2}\,e^{-z_{2}}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})^{p_{2}}\,v_{+}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptz_{2})=(1\!+\!\mathrm{i})2^{-\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}-p_{2}}e^{\frac{\,\pi\tilde{\lambda}\,}{2}}\frac{\,\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}{\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})}\,.

(207b)

With these, we further derive
W_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
as follows:

\displaystyle W_{1}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=\tilde{M}_{\!R}\hskip 0.85358pte^{\frac{\,\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\pi\,}{2}(p_{1}-p_{2})}2^{-2-p_{1}-p_{2}}\frac{\,\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}{\Gamma(2\!+\!p_{1}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})}\frac{\,\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\,}{\Gamma(1\!+\!p_{2}\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\lambda})}\hskip 0.85358pt.

(208)

C.3
Folded-Limit Analysis and the Cutting Rule

In this subsection, we discuss the two closely related aspects of the nonlocal signal:
its behavior in the folded limit and its interpretation in terms of the cutting rule. Both provide useful insight into the physical origin of the oscillatory contribution
as extracted in the squeezed limit.

\blacklozenge
Folded-limit Analysis:

In general, a three-point correlator may be viewed as the folded limit of a four-point correlator,

\lim_{k_{4}\rightarrow 0}\,\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{4})\rangle^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime}\,.

(209)

Once the expression for the corresponding four-point correlator is obtained,
one may directly infer the structure of the three-point correlator
10
;
55
.

{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\mathbf{k}_{4}\rightarrow 0}}

Figure 12
:
From the four-point correlator to the three-point correlator in the folded limit.

To realize this, we introduce the auxiliary coupling through

\Delta\mathcal{L}=\frac{\,-a\,}{\,\Lambda\,}\delta\phi^{\prime}\partial_{\mu}\phi\left(\tilde{N}^{\dagger}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\tilde{N}\right),

(210)

where
\phi^{\prime}\!=\!\text{d}\phi/\text{d}\tau\hskip 0.85358pt
. This allows us to construct a four-point correlator that reduces to the original
three-point correlator in the folded limit, as shown in Fig.
12
. Thus, this four-point correlator can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{4})\rangle^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c=\pm}\!\!\!\!\!a\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358ptc\left(\!\!\frac{\,-\mathrm{i}\,}{\,\Lambda\,}\!\right)^{\!\!3}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\mathrm{d}\tau_{3}\,\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(\mathbf{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\mathcal{F}_{\nu b}(\mathbf{k}_{2},\tau_{2})\mathcal{F}_{\lambda c}(\mathbf{k}_{3},\tau_{3})

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times(-H\tau_{3})^{-1}G_{c}^{\prime}(k_{4},\tau_{3})\!\int\!\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\mathcal{T}_{abc}^{\mu\nu\lambda}\,,

(211)

where
