G_{c}^{\prime}(k_{4},\tau_{3})\!=\!\frac{\,H^{2}\,}{\,2k_{4}\,}\tau_{3}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptc\hskip 0.85358ptk_{4}\tau_{3}}
and

\mathcal{T}_{abc}^{\mu\nu\lambda}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{abc}^{\mu\nu\lambda}=

\displaystyle-\left[\bar{\sigma}^{\mu{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}{\hat{D}_{ab\alpha}}^{\beta}(\mathbf{p}_{12},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\sigma^{\nu}_{\beta{\dot{\beta}}}\check{D}_{bc{\dot{\gamma}}}^{{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{p}_{23},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})\bar{\sigma}^{\lambda{\dot{\gamma}}\gamma}D_{ca\gamma{\dot{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{p}_{31},\tau_{3},\tau_{1})\right]

(212)

\displaystyle-\left[\bar{\sigma}^{\mu{\dot{\alpha}}\alpha}D_{ac\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{p}_{13},\tau_{1},\tau_{3})\bar{\sigma}^{\lambda{\dot{\beta}}\beta}{\hat{D}_{cb\beta}}^{\gamma}(\mathbf{p}_{32},\tau_{3},\tau_{2})\sigma^{\nu}_{\gamma{\dot{\gamma}}}\check{D}_{ba{\dot{\alpha}}}^{{\dot{\gamma}}}(\mathbf{p}_{21},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})\right].

Taking the folded limit, we simplify the new vertex (
210
) for the four-point correlator
to the following factor:

\lim_{k_{4}\rightarrow 0}(-H\tau_{3})^{-1}G_{c}^{\prime}(k_{4},\tau_{3})=-\frac{H}{\,2k_{4}\,}\,.

(213)

Thus, in the folded limit
k_{4}\!\rightarrow\!0\hskip 0.85358pt
, we derive the desired three-point correlator as follows:

\displaystyle\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle^{\prime}

(214)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b,c=\pm}\!\!\!a\hskip 0.85358ptb\hskip 0.85358ptc\left(\!\frac{\,-\mathrm{i}\,}{\,\Lambda\,}\!\right)^{\!\!3}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\mathrm{d}\tau_{3}\,\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(\mathbf{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\mathcal{F}_{\nu b}(\mathbf{k}_{2},\tau_{2})\mathcal{F}_{\lambda c}(\mathbf{k}_{3},\tau_{3})\!\!\int\!\!\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}\,}{\,(2\pi)^{3}\,}\mathcal{T}_{abc}^{\mu\nu\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{k_{4}\rightarrow 0}-\frac{\,2\hskip 0.85358ptk_{4}\,}{\,H\,}\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{4})\rangle^{\prime}\,.

In the literature
57
, the four-point correlator with a scalar triangle loop
was factorized to isolate the nonlocal cosmological-collider signal. In the present study, the factorization of the three-point correlator with a fermion triangle loop
may be viewed as the folded-limit reduction of the corresponding four-point factorization.

\blacklozenge
Cutting Rule Interpretation:

As discussed in Section 2, the nonlocal cosmological-collider signal is extracted
by taking the soft limit for the internal propagators. We refer to such internal propagators as the soft propagators. To illustrate the cutting rule, we first consider a massive scalar field
\sigma

with mode function
y_{k}(\tau)
. Its in-in propagators are given by

\displaystyle D_{\oplus\oplus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=D_{\ominus\oplus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})+D_{\oplus\ominus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1})\hskip 0.85358pt,

(215)

\displaystyle D_{\ominus\ominus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=D_{\oplus\ominus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})+D_{\ominus\oplus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1})\hskip 0.85358pt,

\displaystyle D_{\ominus\oplus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=y_{k}(\tau_{1})y_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt,\hskip 18.49988ptD_{\oplus\ominus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=y_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})y_{k}(\tau_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt.

The nonlocal part of a soft massive propagator satisfies the relations:

D_{\oplus\ominus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})_{\text{(NLoc)}}=D_{\oplus\ominus}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})_{\text{(NLoc)}}=D_{\ominus\oplus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})_{\text{(NLoc)}}.

(216)

In consequence, for the nonlocal signal the
\theta
functions in the time-ordered and
reverse-time-ordered propagators do not affect the time integrals. Thus, the integral

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\!\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\!\!\!\text{d}\tau_{2}

factorizes into a product of the following two separate integrals:

\displaystyle\langle\delta\phi^{n}\rangle

\displaystyle\supset~\sum_{a,b=\pm}\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\!\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\!\!\!\text{d}\tau_{2}\hskip 0.85358ptf_{L}^{a}(\tau_{1})f_{R}^{b}(\tau_{2})D_{ab}(\mathbf{k}_{s},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

(217)

\displaystyle\rightarrow~\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\!\!\!\text{d}\tau_{1}\!\int_{-\infty}^{0^{-}}\!\!\!\!\!\text{d}\tau_{2}\Big[\!\sum_{a,b=\pm}\!\!\!f_{L}^{a}(\tau_{1})f_{R}^{b}(\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\Big]\hskip-0.85358ptD_{\oplus\ominus}(\mathbf{k}_{s},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip 0.85358pt.

A more systematic proof was given in Ref.
57
.

For the fermion propagator
{D}_{ab\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle{D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}\!u_{s}(\tau_{1},k)u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)h_{s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})h_{s,{\dot{\beta}}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,

(218)

\displaystyle{D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s}\!-v_{s}(\tau_{2},k)v_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{1},k)h^{\dagger}_{-s,{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k})h_{-s,\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\hskip 0.85358pt,

\displaystyle{D_{\oplus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle={D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})\!+\!{D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1})\hskip 0.85358pt,

\displaystyle{D_{\ominus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle={D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})\!+\!{D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1})\hskip 0.85358pt.

As shown in the Eq.(
40
) and Eq.(
41
),
we have the following relations:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\left[u_{+}u_{+}^{\dagger}\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!=-\left[v_{+}v_{+}^{\dagger}\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!,\hskip 22.76219pt\left[u_{-}u_{-}^{\dagger}\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!\!=-\left[v_{-}v_{-}^{\dagger}\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!.

(219)

Since
{D}_{ab\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}
is a
2\times 2
matrix carrying the helicity-spinor structure, the nonlocal parts of the time-ordered and reverse-time-ordered propagators are not equal,

{D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})_{\text{(NLoc)}}\neq{D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})_{\text{(NLoc)}}\,.

(220)

Hence, unlike the scalar case, the cutting rule cannot be directly applied to the full spinor propagator.

To overcome this obstacle, we decompose
{D_{\oplus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}

and
{D_{\ominus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}
into symmetric and antisymmetric parts:

\displaystyle{D_{\oplus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\left[\hskip-0.85358ptD^{\mathrm{(S)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\left[\hskip-0.85358ptD^{\mathrm{(A)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]\hskip-0.85358pt\!(\theta_{12}\!-\!\theta_{21})\,,

(221)

\displaystyle{D_{\ominus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\left[\hskip-0.85358ptD^{\mathrm{(S)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\left[\hskip-0.85358ptD^{\mathrm{(A)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]\hskip-0.85358pt\!(\theta_{21}\!-\!\theta_{12})\hskip 0.85358pt,

where we have defined
\theta_{ij}\hskip-0.85358pt=\hskip-0.85358pt\theta(\tau_{i}\!-\!\tau_{j})\hskip 0.85358pt
. The symmetric part is defined as

\left[D^{\mathrm{(S)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]\equiv\frac{1}{\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt}\!\left[{D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt{D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]\!,

(222)

in which by using Eq.(
219
) the nonlocal part can be written as follows:

\left[\hskip-0.85358ptD^{\mathrm{(S)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}}=\frac{\,\sigma_{\!0,\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\,}{2}\!\displaystyle\sum_{s}\!\left[\hskip-0.85358ptu_{s}(\tau_{1},k)u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\hskip-0.85358pt\right]_{\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{(NLoc)}}\hskip-0.85358pt.

(223)

The antisymmetric part is defined as

\left[\hskip-0.85358ptD^{\mathrm{(A)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]\equiv\frac{1}{\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt}\!\left[\hskip-0.85358pt{D_{\ominus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt-\hskip-0.85358pt{D_{\oplus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]\!,

(224)

whose nonlocal part is given by

\left[D^{\mathrm{(A)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}}=\frac{1}{\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt}\!\sum_{s}\!s\hskip-0.85358pt\left[u_{s}(\tau_{1},k)u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\cos\hskip-0.85358pt\theta&~e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\phi}\sin\hskip-0.85358pt\theta\\[2.84526pt]
\!-e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\phi}\hskip-0.85358pt\sin\hskip-0.85358pt\theta&-\cos\hskip-0.85358pt\theta\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}\!.

(225)

In the present analysis, the symmetric parts of
{D_{\oplus\oplus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}

and
{D_{\ominus\ominus}}_{\alpha{\dot{\beta}}}

are included, which allows a cutting-rule-like factorization of the loop diagram. We take this as a conservative estimate since the antisymmetric part
could not be easily treated in this way and its precise treatment
will be pursued for future works.

For the other two types of propagators introduced in Section
2.3
,
the procedure is the same. Their symmetric parts are given by

\displaystyle\left[\hskip-0.85358pt\hat{D}^{\mathrm{(S)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt}\!\left[\hskip-0.85358pt\hat{D}_{\ominus\oplus\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\hat{D}_{\oplus\ominus\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\,\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,}{\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt}\!\sum_{s}\!\left[u_{s}(\tau_{1},k)v_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}},

(226a)

\displaystyle\left[\hskip-0.85358pt\check{D}^{\mathrm{(S)}}_{\hskip 8.04996pt{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt}\!\left[\hskip-0.85358pt\check{D}_{\ominus\oplus{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt+\hskip-0.85358pt\check{D}_{\oplus\ominus{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\hskip-0.85358pt\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\,\epsilon_{{\dot{\alpha}}{\dot{\beta}}}\,}{\hskip 0.85358pt2\hskip 0.85358pt}\!\sum_{s}\!\left[u_{s}^{\dagger}(\tau_{2},k)v_{s}(\tau_{1},k)\right]_{\!\mathrm{(NLoc)}}.

(226b)

C.4
Loop Integration Using Feynman Parameterization

In this subsection, we evaluate the relevant loop-momentum integration
by using Feynman parameterization. This derivation makes the separation between the local UV-sensitive part
and the nonlocal, non-analytic part fairly transparent.

We consider the following master integral,

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bubble}}(k_{s})=\!\!\int\!\!\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}\,}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,|\mathbf{q}|^{-2a}\,|\mathbf{q}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{s}|^{-2b},

(227)

where
a
and
b
are complex parameters in general, and
\mathbf{k}_{s}
denotes an external momentum.

For generic complex factors
a
and
b
, the integral is absolutely convergent
only if the following conditions are obeyed,

\mathrm{Re}(a)\!<\!\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,},\hskip 18.49988pt\mathrm{Re}(b)\!<\!\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,},\hskip 18.49988pt\mathrm{Re}(a\!+\!b)\!>\!\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}.

(228)

Outside the above convergent domain, the integral can be regulated by the analytical continuation. Within the convergent domain, it can be evaluated explicitly using the Feynman parameterization,
and the resultant expression can be analytically continued to the parameter region where
the fermion loop integration is performed.

Using the formula

\frac{1}{A^{a}B^{b}}=\frac{\,\Gamma(a\!+\!b)\,}{\,\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\,}\!\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\,\delta(1\!-\!\xi_{1}\!-\!\xi_{2})\,\frac{\xi_{1}^{a-1}\xi_{2}^{b-1}}{\,(\xi_{1}A\!+\!\xi_{2}B)^{a+b}\,},

(229)

we can reexpress Eq.(
227
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bubble}}(k_{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{\,\Gamma(a\!+\!b)\,}{\,\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\,}\!\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\hskip 0.85358pt\delta(1\!-\!\xi_{1}\!-\!\xi_{2})\!\!\int\hskip-0.85358pt\!\!\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}\,}{\,(2\pi)^{3}\,}\frac{\xi_{1}^{a-1}\xi_{2}^{b-1}}{\,\big[\xi_{1}|\mathbf{q}|^{2}\!+\!\xi_{2}|\mathbf{q}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{s}|^{2}\big]^{a+b}\,}.

(230)

Then, shifting the loop momentum

\mathbf{q}\!=\!\mathbf{p}\hskip-0.85358pt-\hskip-0.85358pt\xi_{2}\mathbf{k}_{s}\hskip 0.85358pt
,
we derive

\,\xi_{1}|\mathbf{q}|^{2}\!+\!\xi_{2}|\mathbf{q}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{s}|^{2}\!=\!p^{2}\!+\!\Delta
with

\Delta\!=\!\xi_{1}\xi_{2}k_{s}^{2}\hskip 0.85358pt
. Thus, the integral (
230
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bubble}}(k_{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{\,\Gamma(a\!+\!b)\,}{\,\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\,}\!\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!\text{d}\xi_{1}\hskip 0.85358pt\text{d}\xi_{2}\hskip 0.85358pt\delta(1\!-\!\xi_{1}\!-\!\xi_{2})\,\xi_{1}^{a-1}\xi_{2}^{b-1}\!\!\!\int\hskip-0.85358pt\!\!\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}\,}{\,(2\pi)^{3}\,}\frac{1}{\,(p^{2}\!+\!\Delta)^{a+b}\,}\,.

(231)

The remaining momentum integral is computed in the standard way,

\int\!\!\hskip-0.85358pt\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{p}\,}{\,(2\pi)^{3}\,}\frac{1}{\,(p^{2}\!+\!\Delta)^{a+b}\,}=\frac{\,\Gamma\!\left(a\!+\!b\!-\!\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\right)\,}{\,(4\pi)^{3/2}\hskip 0.85358pt\Gamma(a\!+\!b)\,}\Delta^{\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}-a-b}.

(232)

Substituting
\Delta\hskip-0.85358pt=\hskip-0.85358pt\xi_{1}\xi_{2}k_{s}^{2}
into the above integral,
we derive the integral
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bubble}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bubble}}(k_{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{\,k_{s}^{\,3-2a-2b}\,}{\,(4\pi)^{3/2}\,}\frac{\,\Gamma\!\left(a\!+\!b\!-\!\frac{3}{2}\right)\,}{\,\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\,}\!\!\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\,\delta(1\!-\!\xi_{1}\!-\!\xi_{2})\,\xi_{1}^{\frac{3}{2}-b-1}\xi_{2}^{\frac{3}{2}-a-1}.

(233)

We compute the Feynman-parameter integral,

\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\,\delta(1\!-\!\xi_{1}\!-\!\xi_{2})\,\xi_{1}^{\alpha}\xi_{2}^{\beta}=\frac{\,\Gamma(1\!+\!\alpha)\Gamma(1\!+\!\beta)\,}{\Gamma(2\!+\!\alpha\!+\!\beta)}\,,

(234)

which leads to

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bubble}}(k_{s})=\frac{\,\Gamma\!\left(a\!+\!b\!-\!\frac{3}{2}\right)\Gamma\!\left(\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\!-\!b\right)\Gamma\!\left(\frac{\,3\,}{\,2\,}\!-\!a\right)\,}{(4\pi)^{3/2}\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\Gamma(3-a-b)}k_{s}^{\,3-2a-2b}.

(235)

We note that the integral (
235
) is first derived in the convergence domain
(
228
), and then it can be obtained for other domains by analytic continuation.

It is useful to compare this analytic-continuation prescription with other regularization schemes. If we introduce a sharp UV cutoff,
|\mathbf{q}|\!<\!\Lambda_{\mathrm{UV}}
, then the large-
q
expansion
yields the following:

\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}(k_{s})=\frac{\Lambda_{\mathrm{UV}}^{\,3-2a-2b}}{\,2\pi^{2}(3\!-\!2a\!-\!2b)\,}\!+\!\frac{~b(2b\!-\!1)k_{s}^{2}\Lambda_{\mathrm{UV}}^{\,1-2a-2b}\,}{\,6\pi^{2}(1\!-\!2a\!-\!2b)\,}+\cdots+\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{NLoc}}(k_{s})\,,

(236)

where the omitted terms are higher-order analytic functions of
k_{s}^{2}\hskip 0.85358pt
. These cutoff-dependent terms can be absorbed into counterterms as generated by local operators. Then the remaining non-analytic part,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{NLoc}}(k_{s})
,
just corresponds to Eq.(
235
) and
is independent of regularization scheme,
which contributes to the cosmological collider signal.

With the dimensional regularization, we continue the spatial dimension to
D\!=\!3\!-\!\epsilon

and considers

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bubble}}^{(D)}=\mu^{\epsilon}\hskip-0.85358pt\!\!\int\!\!\!\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{D}\mathbf{q}\,}{(2\pi)^{D}}\,(\mathbf{q}^{2})^{-a}\big[(\mathbf{q}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{s})^{2}\big]^{-b}.

(237)

Using the standard massless two-point integral, we have

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bubble}}^{(D)}=\frac{\,\mu^{\epsilon}(k_{s}^{2})^{\frac{\,D\,}{\,2\,}-a-b}\,}{\,(4\pi)^{D/2}\,}\frac{\,\Gamma\!\left(\frac{\,D\,}{\,2\,}\!-\!a\right)\Gamma\!\left(\frac{\,D\,}{\,2\,}\!-\!b\right)\Gamma\!\left(a\!+\!b\!-\!\frac{\,D\,}{\,2\,}\right)\,}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\Gamma(D\!-\!a\!-\!b)}.

(238)

In this scheme, the power-divergent analytic terms do not appear as explicit
1/\epsilon
poles. This is expected for the dimensional regularization:
the power-divergent analytic contributions do appear,
whereas the non-analytic momentum dependence is retained.

The important point is that different regularization schemes affect only the analytic UV-sensitive part
of the bubble integral. In the cutoff scheme, these appear as analytic terms such as
those shown in Eq.(
236
), whereas in the dimensional regularization
the corresponding power-divergent analytic terms do not exist. In contrast, the non-analytic dependence on
k_{s}
is captured by the expression
(
235
) which is obtained from the analytic continuation.

It is useful to clarify in what sense the non-analytic part is associated with the soft region. Strictly speaking, the full loop integral is not dominated by low loop-momentum in a naive sense. Instead, the nonlocal cosmological-collider signal is generated by the kinematic region in which the internal momentum
becomes comparable to the external soft scale, or, equivalently in which the two internal propagators become soft as

k_{s}\!\!\rightarrow\!0

57
. In this region, the loop integral develops the non-analytic dependence on the momentum ratio that gives rise to
the oscillatory cosmological-collider signal. In contrast, the hard-momentum region admits an ordinary expansion
in the soft external momentum and thus contributes only analytic functions of
k_{s}^{2}
. But, this does not mean that one could simply discard the hard-momentum region and impose a soft cutoff on the integral
to estimate the nonlocal contribution. Once a regulator or matching scale is introduced, the soft contribution generally
contains scheme-dependent pieces, so the universal non-analytic part must be isolated by a consistent subtraction or
matching procedure. In this sense, the oscillatory cosmological-collider signal is encoded in the soft-propagator region
of the bubble integral, whereas the hard-momentum region, although not generating the signal itself, is still needed
to organize the decomposition and remove the scheme-dependence of intermediate expressions.

Appendix D
Brief Review of Cosmological Collider Physics

In this Appendix, we give a brief review of the cosmological collider physics
and define the relevant observables and notations used in the main text.

During inflation, the inflaton field can be decomposed into a homogeneous background
\phi_{0}(t)
and quantum fluctuations
\delta\phi(t,\mathbf{x})
:

\phi(t,\mathbf{x})=\phi_{0}(t)\!+\!\delta\phi(t,\mathbf{x})\,.

(239)

The background field
\phi_{0}(t)
slowly rolls during inflation. On the other hand, the fluctuation
\delta\phi
can be treated as an approximately massless scalar field due to the flatness of the inflaton potential. As a result, in single-field inflation the quantum evolution of
\delta\phi(t,\mathbf{x})
is nearly Gaussian.

Figure 13
:
An illustrative diagram of a four-point correlator of the inflaton
\delta\phi
with the exchange of a massive scalar
\sigma
, namely
\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{4})\rangle
. The horizontal line represents the future boundary of de Sitter spacetime,
\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}
, and the two shaded blobs denote the interaction vertices.

However, the inflaton needs to interact with other fields because reheating requires the inflaton decays through the inflation couplings with the Standard Model (SM) fields. Such interactions can generate non-Gaussianity in the inflaton fluctuations, characterized by their
n
-point (
n\!\hskip-0.85358pt\geqslant\!3\hskip 0.85358pt
) correlators evaluated at the future boundary of inflationary spacetime,

\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdots\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{n})\rangle\equiv\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}}\langle\delta\phi(\tau,\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\tau,\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdots\delta\phi(\tau,\mathbf{k}_{n})\rangle\,,

(240)

namely in the late-time limit
\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}
. A schematic example is shown in Fig.
13
.

In the spatially flat gauge, the primordial curvature perturbation
\zeta
is related to the inflaton fluctuation by

\zeta\simeq-\frac{\,H\,}{\,\dot{\phi_{0}}\,}\delta\phi(\mathbf{x})\,.

(241)

Consequently, primordial non-Gaussianity, as characterized by the
n
-point correlators of the curvature perturbation
\zeta
, can be directly probed by large-scale-structure observations and therefore encodes information about interactions during inflation. The corresponding
n
-point correlators of the inflaton can be computed in the well-known in-in formalism
69
;
70
, or equivalently in the Schwinger-Keldysh (SK) path-integral formalism. See
27
;
68
;
21
for reviews.

Depending on the interactions and on the nature of the particles involved, the resulting non-Gaussianity can exhibit a variety of shapes. Among the different types of primordial non-Gaussianity, cosmological collider signals have attracted particular attention in recent years
25
;
26
;
14
;
53
;
51
;
33
;
10
;
19
;
40
;
22
;
38
;
39
. A cosmological collider (CC) signal, also known as a clock signal, is an oscillatory contribution to the
n
-point correlators

\langle\delta\phi^{n}\rangle
in a special kinematic regime. For instance, the CC signal for a three-point correlator
\langle\delta\phi^{3}\rangle
is encoded in its squeezed limit:

\displaystyle\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle_{\text{squeezed}}

\displaystyle\thicksim c_{1}f_{\mathrm{BG}}(\mathbf{k})\!+\!c_{2}f_{\mathrm{CC}}(\mathbf{k})\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{k})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\omega}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\varphi}\!+\!\mathrm{H.c.}

(242)

In the above,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are constant coefficients,
\omega
is the oscillation frequency, and
\varphi
denotes the relevant phase angle. The first term,
f_{\text{BG}}(\mathbf{k})
, denotes the background non-Gaussian contribution, which is analytic in the momenta
\mathbf{k}
and typically takes the form of a low-order polynomial. In contrast, the second term,
f_{\text{CC}}(\mathbf{k})\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{k})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\omega}
, represents the CC signal. In Eq.(
242
),
f_{\text{CC}}(\mathbf{k})
is also analytic in the momenta, whereas
\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{k})
denotes a function of the momenta, typically a ratio of two characteristic scales, such as

k_{\mathrm{long}}/k_{\mathrm{short}}
. The CC signal originates from the non-analytic factor
\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{k})^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\omega}
.

Further, for the above correlator with a CC signal, the squeezed limit is a special momentum configuration which describes a situation with a clear separation of scales, a long-wavelength mode
k_{\text{long}}
and a short-wavelength mode
k_{\text{short}}
satisfying
k_{\text{long}}\ll k_{\text{short}}
.

For a four-point correlator with the momentums satisfying
\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{4})\rangle
, with
\mathbf{k}_{s}\equiv\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}
, the
squeezed limit
refers to the kinematic configuration
k_{s}\!\ll\!k_{1}+k_{2},k_{3}+k_{4}\hskip 0.85358pt
, as shown in the Fig.
14
. The long-wavelength (soft) mode
k_{\text{long}}
corresponds to
k_{s}
, while the short-wavelength (hard) modes
k_{\text{short}}
corresponds to
k_{1},\,k_{2},\,k_{3}
and
k_{4}
.

For a three-point correlator

\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle
, the
squeezed limit
refers to the configuration
k_{1}\ll k_{2}\simeq k_{3}
, as shown in the Fig.
15
. The long-wavelength mode
k_{\text{long}}
corresponds to
k_{3}
, while the short-wavelength modes
k_{\text{short}}
corresponds to
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
.

The squeezed limit could cleanly separate the oscillation CC signal away from analytic contributions from contact interactions and local operators, which are typically smooth power law dependence of the momentum
\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{k})^{n}
.

D.1
Origin of the Cosmological Collider Signatures

In general, cosmological collider signals arise from interactions between the inflaton and massive particles with masses
m\gtrsim H
. As a result, the masses and spins of such particles can be encoded in the resulting CC signals
25
;
10
. In this respect, an analogy may be drawn with particle accelerators in flat spacetime, where the presence of intermediate particles is revealed through characteristic structures of scattering amplitudes. For illustration, we give a diagram showing a
four-point correlator of the inflaton mediated by a massive scalar boson
\sigma
.

To study cosmological collider signals, it is essential to understand the behavior of massive fields propagating in the bulk spacetime of de Sitter, where the conformal time lies in the range of
-\infty<\tau<0
. For a free massive field, the nontrivial de Sitter geometry generally leads to mode functions expressed in terms of special functions, such as Hankel or Whittaker functions. For concreteness, we consider the scalar field
\sigma
with mass
m
. Its mode function satisfies the equation of motion,

\ddot{\sigma}_{k}\!+\!3H\dot{\sigma}_{k}\!+\!\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}\sigma_{k}\!+\!m^{2}\sigma_{k}=0\,,

(243)

where
\sigma_{k}
denotes the mode function,
H
is the Hubble parameter during inflation, and
a(\tau)=-1/(H\tau)
is the scale factor. The solution of Eq.(
243
) is given by

\sigma_{k}(\tau)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi\,}H}{2}e^{-\pi\tilde{\nu}/2}(-\tau)^{3/2}\text{H}_{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}^{(1)}(-k\tau)\,,

(244)

where
\tilde{\nu}=\sqrt{(m/H)^{2}\!-\!9/4\,}
,
and
\text{H}_{n}^{(1)}(z)

denotes the Hankel function of the first kind. In the Schwinger-Keldysh (SK) formalism, the bulk propagators of this field take
the following form
21
:

\displaystyle D_{\oplus\oplus}\left(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\,\theta\left(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2}\right)\!+\!\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma_{k}(\tau_{2})\,\theta\left(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1}\right),

(245a)

\displaystyle D_{\ominus\oplus}\left(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma_{k}(\tau_{2}),

(245b)

\displaystyle D_{\oplus\ominus}\left(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{2}),

(245c)

\displaystyle D_{\ominus\ominus}\left(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma_{k}(\tau_{2})\,\theta\left(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2}\right)\!+\!\sigma_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\,\theta\left(\tau_{2}\!-\!\tau_{1}\right),

(245d)

where
\sigma_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{2})
and
its complex conjugate are given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi H^{2}}{4}e^{-\pi\tilde{\nu}}\left(\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\right)^{3/2}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}^{(1)}(-k\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}^{*}}^{(2)}(-k\tau_{2}),

(246a)

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\sigma_{k}(\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi H^{2}}{4}e^{-\pi\tilde{\nu}}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{3/2}\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}^{*}}^{(1)}(-k\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}^{(2)}(-k\tau_{2}).

(246b)

For the present study of the cosmological collider signals, it is useful to make it clear on how the relevant information is encoded in the propagator of a massive field. The key feature is the non-analytic behavior of the propagator, which contains an imaginary power-law dependence on time and can be extracted in the late-time limit
|k\tau_{1}|,|k\tau_{2}|\rightarrow 0^{-}
. This is also referred to the soft limit of the propagator. In this limit, the propagator can be decomposed into two contributions relevant for the cosmological collider signal, known as the nonlocal and local parts
63
,
for instance,

\lim_{|k\tau_{1}|,|k\tau_{2}|\rightarrow 0^{-}}\!\!D_{\oplus\ominus}(k,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\left[D_{\oplus\ominus}(k,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}\!+\!\left[D_{\oplus\ominus}(k,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(Loc)}}\,,

(247)

where the
\left[D_{\oplus\ominus}(k,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}
and
\left[D_{\oplus\ominus}(k,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(Loc)}}
are given by

\displaystyle\left[D_{\oplus\ominus}(k,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(NLoc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\,H^{2}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{3/2}\,}{4\pi}\Bigg[\big[\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}\left(\!\frac{\,k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\,}{4}\!\right)^{\!\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\!\!+\big[\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\big]^{2}\left(\!\frac{\,k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\,}{4}\!\right)^{\!\!-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\Bigg],

(248a)

\displaystyle\left[D_{\oplus\ominus}(k,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\text{(Loc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\,H^{2}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{3/2}\,}{4\pi}\Gamma(-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Gamma(\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu})\Bigg[e^{\pi\tilde{\nu}}\left(\!\frac{\,\tau_{1}\,}{\tau_{2}}\!\right)^{\!\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\!\!+e^{-\pi\tilde{\nu}}\left(\!\frac{\,\tau_{1}\,}{\tau_{2}}\!\right)^{\!\!-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\Bigg].

(248b)

The nonlocal and local parts have distinct physical interpretations. First, the nonlocal part is non-analytic in
k
. In particular, the term proportional to
k^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}}
gives rise, after Fourier transformation, to a long-range correlation of the form

|\mathbf{x}_{1}\hskip-0.85358pt-\mathbf{x}_{2}|^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt2\tilde{\nu}}
. By contrast, the local part is independent of
k
, and its Fourier transform yields a contact term proportional to

\delta^{3}(\mathbf{x}_{1}\!-\hskip-0.85358pt\mathbf{x}_{2})
, as expected for a local contribution. Second, the time dependence of the nonlocal part reflects the accumulated dynamical phase of two propagating particles, whereas the time dependence of the local part corresponds to the dynamical phase of a single particle propagating from
\tau_{2}
to
\tau_{1}
(or from
\tau_{1}
to
\tau_{2}
, with an additional suppression factor
e^{-2\pi\tilde{\nu}}
).

For the inflaton fluctuation, treated as a massless scalar field in the de Sitter spacetime, the mode function takes the form:

\delta\phi_{k}(\tau)=\frac{H}{\,\sqrt{2k^{3}\,}\,}(1\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau)e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau}\,.

(249)

The corresponding bulk-to-bulk propagator is
given by

G_{\oplus\ominus}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\delta\phi_{k}(\tau_{1})\delta\phi_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})=\frac{\,H^{2}\,}{\,2k^{3}\,}\left[1\!+\hskip-0.85358pt\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk(\tau_{1}\!-\!\tau_{2})\!+\!k^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\right]e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk(\tau_{1}\hskip-0.85358pt-\tau_{2})}\,.

(250)

Since we evaluate the correlator
\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdots\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{n})\rangle
in the late-time limit
\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}
, we also need the bulk-to-boundary propagators of the inflaton
\delta\phi
, for which one time leg, say
\tau_{2}
, is taken to the final time slice, namely,
\tau_{2}=\tau_{\!f}\rightarrow 0^{-}
. They are defined as

G_{a}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1})\equiv\lim_{\tau_{\!f}\rightarrow 0}G_{a\oplus}(\mathbf{k};\tau_{1},\tau_{\!f})\hskip 0.85358pt.

(251)

Then, the plus- and minus-type bulk-to-boundary propagators take the following form:

\displaystyle G_{\oplus}(\mathbf{k},\tau)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau_{\!f}\rightarrow 0}\frac{H^{2}}{\,2k^{3}\,}\left[1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk(\tau\!-\!\tau_{\!f})\!+\!k^{2}\tau\tau_{\!f}\right]e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk(\tau-\tau_{\!f})}=\frac{H^{2}}{\,2k^{3}\,}\left[1\!-\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau\right]e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau}\hskip 0.85358pt,

(252a)

\displaystyle G_{\ominus}(\mathbf{k},\tau)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau_{\!f}\rightarrow 0}\frac{H^{2}}{\,2k^{3}\,}\left[1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk(\tau\!-\!\tau_{\!f})\!+\!k^{2}\tau\tau_{\!f}\right]e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk(\tau-\tau_{\!f})}=\frac{H^{2}}{\,2k^{3}\,}\left[1\!+\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau\right]e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau}\hskip 0.85358pt.

(252b)

Hence, in the late-time limit, the massive field oscillates as

\tau^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\!\sim\!e^{-\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}Ht}
, since in de Sitter spacetime the conformal time and cosmic time are related by

\tau\!=\!-H^{-1}e^{-Ht}
, whereas the massless inflaton mode oscillates as
e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau}
. During inflation, interactions between the inflaton and the heavy field can give rise to a resonance production or decay of the heavy particle
19
;
63
. For computing the correlator induced by such a heavy field, one needs to deal with the following integral:

\int\!\text{d}\tau\,\tau^{\alpha}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau}\tau^{\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\sim\frac{\,\sqrt{2\pi\tilde{\nu}\,}\,}{k}e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\frac{\pi}{4}}\tau_{*}^{\alpha}e^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358ptk\tau_{*}}\tau_{*}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}\,,

(253)

where
\alpha
is a constant, and the dominant contribution comes from the stationary point
\tau_{*}
satisfying
|k\tau_{*}|\!\simeq\!\tilde{\nu}
. Physically,
\tau_{*}
corresponds to the resonance time, at which a resonant process takes place, such as the decay of a massive particle into massless inflaton modes, or, conversely the production of a heavy particle from inflaton modes through the interaction. Through this resonance, the oscillatory behavior of the heavy-particle mode function, characterized by
\tau^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}
, is transferred to the oscillatory behavior of the cosmological collider signal, which then appears as a non-analytic momentum dependence of the form
k^{\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}
, as indicated in Eq.(
242
).

As an on-shell effect, the cosmological collider signal may be viewed as the inflationary analog of the mass-pole structure associated with Mandelstam variables in the flat-spacetime scattering amplitudes. One of its key properties, namely the oscillation frequency
\omega
in Eq.(
242
), is insensitive to the detailed form of the couplings between the inflaton and the heavy field, and depends primarily on the heavy-particle mass,
\omega=\tilde{\nu}\!\sim\!m/H
. For this reason, the cosmological collider signals provide a nearly model-independent probe of very massive particles in the early universe.

In contrast, the overall size of the signal, namely the coefficient

c_{2}f_{\mathrm{CC}}(\mathbf{k})

in Eq.(
242
), generally does depend on the details of the interaction and thus can carry complementary information about the coupling structure. We will return to this point in the following sections.

D.2
Local and Nonlocal Cosmological Collider Signatures

As discussed in the previous section, the late-time behavior of the propagator for a massive field can be separated into two parts, namely the local and nonlocal contributions, as shown in Eq.(
248a
). Correspondingly, cosmological collider signals can also be classified into two types: local signals and nonlocal signals.

The most commonly studied example is the
four-point correlator,

\langle\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{1})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{2})\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{3})

\delta\phi(\mathbf{k}_{4})\rangle
,
with the momentum conservation

\mathbf{k}_{1}\!+\mathbf{k}_{2}\!+\mathbf{k}_{3}\!+\mathbf{k}_{4}\!=0\hskip 0.85358pt
. We further define the following:

\mathbf{k}_{s}\equiv\mathbf{k}_{1}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{2},\qquad r_{1}\equiv k_{s}/k_{12},\qquad r_{2}\equiv k_{s}/k_{34}\,.

(254)

For the four-point correlator,
the squeezed limit is given by

k_{s}\!\ll\!k_{12},k_{34}\hskip 0.85358pt
,
or equivalently,
r_{1},r_{2}\!\ll\!1\hskip 0.85358pt
. This squeezed configuration is illustrated in Fig.
14
, where the blue momentum vector
\mathbf{k}_{s}
is highly compressed.

Figure 14
:
Illustration of the momentum configuration of the four-point correlator
in the squeezed limit.

The local and nonlocal signals exhibit different momentum dependences in the
four-point correlator. For the nonlocal signal, the function
\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{k})

in Eq.(
242
) takes
the form of

\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{\,k_{s}^{2}\,}{k_{12}k_{34}}=r_{1}r_{2}\,.

(255)

In contrast, for the local signal we have

\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{\,k_{34}\,}{k_{12}}=\frac{\,r_{1}\,}{r_{2}}\,.

(256)

In general, the squeezed-limit behavior of the four-point correlator can be written as follows:

\langle\delta\phi^{4}\rangle_{\text{squeezed}}\thicksim A_{\mathrm{NLoc}}\left(r_{1},r_{2}\right)\left(r_{1}r_{2}\right)^{\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\omega}+A_{\mathrm{Loc}}(r_{1},r_{2})\!\left(\!\frac{\,r_{1}\,}{r_{2}\!}\right)^{\!\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\omega}\!\!+A_{\mathrm{BG}}(r_{1},r_{2})\,,

(257)

where
A_{\mathrm{NLoc}}
,
A_{\mathrm{Loc}}
, and
A_{\mathrm{BG}}
are analytic functions of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}\hskip 0.85358pt
.

The local and nonlocal signals have distinctive physical origins. This distinction can be understood most transparently
from the resonance picture discussed in Section
D.1
. In this picture, the nonlocal signal originates from the gravitational production of
a pair of massive particles, followed by their resonant decay into inflaton modes
at separated interaction vertices. This contribution is associated with the nonlocal part of the massive-field propagator,

\left[D_{\oplus\ominus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\mathrm{NLoc}}\sim\left(\!\frac{\,k_{s}^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\,}{4}\!\right)^{\!\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}.

(258)

After imposing the resonance conditions at the two interaction vertices,
this time dependence is mapped into the momentum-space clock signal,

\left(\!\frac{k_{s}^{2}}{\,k_{12}k_{34}\,}\!\right)^{\!\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}.

(259)

By contrast, the local signal corresponds to the resonant production and
subsequent decay of a single massive particle at interaction vertices. It is associated with the local part of the massive-field propagator,

\left[D_{\oplus\ominus}(\mathbf{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\right]_{\mathrm{Loc}}\sim\left(\!\frac{\,\tau_{1}\,}{\,\tau_{2}\,}\!\right)^{\!\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}.

(260)

The resonance conditions then map this time dependence into the local clock signal,

\left(\!\frac{\,k_{12}\,}{\,k_{34}\,}\!\right)^{\!\!\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\tilde{\nu}}.

(261)

Hence, the local and nonlocal signals arise from different bulk histories,
and their oscillatory phases track different intervals of massive-particle propagation
in the inflationary bulk. Most previous studies have focused on extracting the nonlocal signals in the
four-point correlator.

Compared with the four-point case, the structure of the three-point signal is
more subtle. A useful way to understand it is to connect
the three-point correlator to the folded limit of the four-point correlator by taking

k_{4}\rightarrow 0
. In this limit, the momentum ratio

r_{2}\!=\hskip-0.85358ptk_{s}/k_{34}
approaches unity,

\lim_{k_{4}\rightarrow 0}r_{2}=\lim_{k_{4}\rightarrow 0}\frac{\,k_{s}\,}{k_{34}}=\lim_{k_{4}\rightarrow 0}\frac{\,|\mathbf{k}_{3}\!+\!\mathbf{k}_{4}|\,}{k_{3}\!+\!k_{4}}=1\,.

(262)

In consequence, both the local and nonlocal contributions become functions of the same momentum ratio
r_{1}=k_{3}/k_{12}
. In particular, after taking the folded limit, both contributions exhibit the same characteristic non-analytic dependence,
r_{1}^{\pm i\omega}
. Hence, unlike in the four-point correlator, the local and nonlocal signals in the three-point correlator can no longer be distinguished from their squeezed-limit momentum-dependence alone.

Accordingly, the squeezed-limit behavior of the three-point correlator can be written as

\langle\delta\phi^{3}\rangle_{\text{squeezed}}\thicksim B_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{NL}}\left(r_{1}\right)\,r_{1}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\omega}\!+\!B_{\mathrm{BG}}\left(r_{1}\right)\,,

(263)

where
B_{\mathrm{loc},\mathrm{NLoc}}
and
B_{\mathrm{BG}}
are analytic functions of
r_{1}
. In the above,
B_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{NL}}
denotes the overall amplitude of the cosmological collider signal, which in general receives contributions from both the local and nonlocal parts. The squeezed limit of the three-point correlator corresponds to

r_{1}=\frac{k_{3}}{\,k_{12}\,}\rightarrow 0\,,

(264)

which is illustrated in Fig.
15
.

Figure 15
:
The momentum configuration of the three-point correlator in the squeezed limit.

Nevertheless, the local and nonlocal contributions remain physically and calculationally distinct. They may be defined separately by extracting the local or nonlocal part of the massive propagator, or equivalently by taking the folded limit of the corresponding local and nonlocal pieces of the four-point correlator. What is lost in the three-point limit is not the distinction in their physical origins, but only the kinematic distinction in their final momentum-dependence.
